        I predict that the newspaper will delve into the same topics that the news speaks about nowadays, but adjusted for the time. It will most likely contain a mixture of political, economic, local, and social news. For example, there may be a discussion of the upcoming elections and the likelihood of a certain candidate winning. It may also touch on the state of the economy as well as local events and happenings. Many of the common terms may be from a political context. These may include something like "Congress," "President," "Senate, " "House, " "government, " "law, " and "people." Since this takes place in Massachusetts, which includes a major shipping town, it may be common for words like "ship, " "trade, " "business, " "market, " "port, " and "vessel." Cities and towns within Massachusetts will likely also be mentioned.

    Source line 471

        I initially thought that the newspaper would pertain to topics relevant to Massachusetts, such as local and national politics, based on the location of the publication. This was the case, and news related to European happenings was far less prevalent. One of the topics that I examined was strongly associated with words like "French,” “France,” “government,” “Paris,” “Assembly,” “England,” “Ireland,” “war,” “troops,” and “army.” The highly weighted documents seem to support that interpretation of this topic, which was the least weighted topic in the corpus. There were many topics related to local happenings in Massachusetts as well as political disturbances. This was similar to my prediction in the way that there were often political elements in the documents, but the specific political context was sometimes different. The focus seemed to be more on American politics for the most part, which was expected.

    This was not surprising to me because of my presupposition about the context of the news, but it could make sense in the context that the United States was not necessarily as involved on the world stage at this time. These text mining techniques allowed me to have a much easier time seeing these trends without having to read the documents individually. Using the articles and keywords together allowed me to create a much fuller mental picture of the document's common topics. Overall, it appears that local news was very prevalent in the 1830s news cycle.

        ## Part 2 — Select a newspaper

    I selected two newspapers to compare Boston with Gloucester, a fishing town:

    * `BOSTON_COURIER_MARINE_JOURNAL__sn84023118.jsonl`: the *Boston Courier*, Boston, 1851 and 1858.
    * `Telegraph_and_News__sn83020197.jsonl`: the *Telegraph and News*, Gloucester, 1852–53 and 1858–59.

    Source line 52

        ## Part 3a — Make predictions

    These predictions are based on the newspapers' names, locations, and dates.

    **Boston Courier.** I expect shipping reports, trade and market prices, national politics, foreign news,
    and city news. Likely words include *ship, arrived, sailed, port, cargo, market, price, bank, election,
    congress, slavery,* and *union*.

    **Telegraph and News.** I expect fishing to be a major subject, along with town meetings, schools,
    churches, temperance, and state politics. Likely words include *fish, mackerel, cod, schooner, fishing,
    crew, town, school, church, liquor,* and *law*.

    **Both papers.** I expect advertisements, legal notices, marriages and deaths, editorials, stories,
    and poems. Common words might include *state, city, house, time, day, people,* and *dollars*, along
    with dates and abbreviations such as *capt, inst,* and *ult*. Scanning errors may produce words such
    as *tho* and *tbe*.

    Source line 102

        ## Part 3b — Train a topic model

    ### Prepare the text

    Each dataset row contains a newspaper page. I split pages at headings marked with `#` and at paragraph
    breaks around 3,000 characters, then merge pieces shorter than 800 characters. These pieces are only
    approximations of articles: one piece can contain several short items or part of a long story.

    I remove HTML tags while keeping the text inside them, and discard pieces with fewer than 20 words
    of at least three letters. The table below shows how many pieces remain in each newspaper.

    Source line 187

        ### Stoplist and training

    A stoplist removes common words such as *the* and *and*. The first model uses 30 topics, 500 training
    iterations, and words that appear in at least five articles. I use a fixed random seed so the results
    can be repeated. The notebook saves model results locally to avoid repeating unchanged training.

    Source line 284

        ## Part 4 — Analyze

    I use SQL to examine each topic's top words and the articles with the highest topic percentages.
    In the overview, `corpus_pct` estimates a topic's share of the collection, giving longer articles more
    weight. Topic numbers are labels, not rankings, and are specific to each model.

    Source line 308

        **Topic 29: stories and poetry.** This topic averages 9.57% of a Gloucester article and 1.08% of a
    Boston article. Here are its top words and ten articles with the highest topic percentages:

    Source line 346

        The top articles are stories and poems from the *Telegraph and News*, mostly from the front page.
    Some pieces belong to the same story, because the page-splitting step divides long stories.

    **Topic 8: repeated scanning text.** Its top words mix *choice gifts*, *burrill*, *warrants*, and *sims*.
    The articles help explain this unusual combination:

    Source line 387

        Many of these articles repeat the same words or paragraphs. For example, a bookseller's advertisement
    repeats *CHOICE OF GIFTS* hundreds of times. Other examples repeat parts of a committee list and a
    legal document. The repeated text dominates this topic, so I clean it in Part 5.

    The next table compares average topic percentages between the two newspapers. Each article counts
    equally here, unlike the length-weighted collection overview.

    Source line 418

        **What the first model shows.** Boston has more shipping and market reports. Gloucester has more
    stories, poems, and probate notices. Topics can reflect a type of writing, such as dialogue or a
    shipping list, as well as a subject. Repeated scanning text also creates misleading topics, and
    common words such as *new* and *boston* appear in several topics.

    Source line 429

        ## Part 5 — Iterate

    ### Remove repeated scanning text

    Before changing the model settings, I clean repeated text by:

    1. Rejoining words split by a hyphen and a space, such as *men- tioned*.
    2. Keeping only the first copy of each identical line on a page.
    3. Dropping lines with at least 50 words when fewer than a fifth of the words are different.

    This removes about 6% of the words. It may also remove genuine repetition, and it does not catch
    every scanning error. The tables show the overall change and some affected words.

    Source line 496

        ### Retrain after cleaning

    I keep 30 topics and the original stoplist so I can see the effect of cleaning alone.

    Source line 514

        After cleaning, repeated words such as *gifts* and *burrill* no longer dominate the top-word lists.
    However, common words such as *may, time,* and *new* still lead several topics.

    ### Add words to the stoplist

    This query finds words that appear among the top 20 words in at least four topics.

    Source line 542

        I add general words such as *new, may, time, first,* and *great*, along with date abbreviations such
    as *th, inst,* and *ult*. I keep place names such as *boston* and *gloucester* because they help
    identify the places discussed in an article. The full list of added words is below.

    Source line 563

    Source line 579

    Source line 602

        The added words no longer lead topics. The results now include more specific words, and fish-market
    reports form a separate topic from cotton and flour markets. Changing the stoplist can reorganize
    topics as well as remove words.

    ### Change the number of topics

    With the same cleaned articles and expanded stoplist, I also train models with 10 and 60 topics.

    Source line 632

        **10 topics:** The topics cover broad categories, such as fiction, politics, markets, and shipping.
    Fishing is mixed with other news, markets, and ship lists instead of forming its own topic.

    **30 topics:** The topics are more specific than in the 10-topic model, with separate fish-market
    reports and other recognizable types of content.

    **60 topics:** More specific subjects appear, including slavery and federal law (21), fishing news
    (27), and the López expedition to Cuba (44). Some topics are dominated by repeated advertisements
    or lists of names. More topics therefore do not always produce more useful results.

    I use the 60-topic model for a closer look at these subjects. The following table compares the
    average topic percentages in the two newspapers.

    Source line 669

        Here are the three articles with the highest percentages for topics 21 (slavery and federal law),
    27 (fishing), and 44 (the López expedition). This checks the interpretations against the actual text.

    Source line 693

        ## Part 6 — Discuss

    **What I found.** The Boston Courier contains shipping lists, market reports, politics, and foreign
    news. The Telegraph and News includes fishing reports, but also many stories, poems, advertisements,
    and legal notices. Topics often describe a type of writing rather than a single subject.

    **Compared with my predictions.** Shipping and trade were prominent in Boston, as I expected.
    Gloucester had the fishing content I predicted, including reports about fishing grounds and disputes
    with British cruisers. However, fiction was more prominent than I expected. The final model also
    separated slavery and federal law, while temperance did not emerge as a clear topic of its own.
    That does not mean temperance was absent from the papers.

    **Most surprising.** Repeated scanning text strongly affected the first model. A topic that looked
    important could be mostly one advertisement or paragraph repeated many times. I also found coverage
    of the López expedition to Cuba, which I had not predicted.

    **How the methods helped.** Top-word lists gave an overview, and reading the articles with the highest
    topic percentages helped explain those lists. Cleaning repeated text and extending the stoplist made
    the topics easier to interpret. Comparing 10, 30, and 60 topics showed how larger topic counts can
    separate specific subjects but also create topics dominated by a single advertisement or notice.

    **Limitations.** Splitting pages can divide one story into several pieces, so several top results may
    come from one original article. Cleaning can remove genuine repetition and miss other errors. The
    papers also cover different sets of years, so differences between them may reflect the dates sampled
    as well as their locations. Topic words and percentages need to be checked against the articles.

        ### Baseline interpretation

    At the default settings, the baseline mixes public affairs and business in
    topic 0, and society and hotels in topic 3. Rail travel (5), visitor listings
    (6), theatre (8), and fashion (9) are more recognizable. Topic 1 is largest
    at 21.02%, but broad words such as “may,” “life,” and “people” make it hard
    to label. Topic 4 mixes market vocabulary with HTML tags such as “td” and
    “tr,” showing how source formatting can influence a topic.

    Source line 567

        ## Part 5: Iterate

    I compare the 10-topic baseline with 20 topics, then add “miss,” “little,”
    “great,” “good,” “time,” “know,” and “don” to the example's stoplist. These
    are common titles or broad narrative words. Removing them may clarify
    subjects, but also hides some information about style and social conventions.

    Source line 602

        ## Part 6: Discuss

    ### Findings and predictions

    The results show public affairs, commerce, society news, entertainment,
    travel, fashion, and horse racing. “SOUTHERN STATE GOVERNMENTS NOT ABOLISHED
    BY THE WAR” (document 2739, February 21, 1868) supports my Reconstruction
    prediction but there isnt an overarching theme found by the model.
    Boston, government, and war appear in the topic words, but
    industrialization is not a clear separate topic.

    ### Unexpected findings

    The hotel coverage and visitor directories were more prominent than I
    expected. Source artifacts were also surprisingly influential: document
    1978 contains HTML table tags, while document 39662 repeatedly prints
    “OLIVETTE.” Their high topic weights do not make them representative articles.

    ### Topic modeling and understanding the corpus

    LDA made the collection easier to explore, and SQL queries connected topic
    words to actual articles. More topics exposed useful distinctions, but
    merging short segments sometimes combined unrelated items. Repetition,
    formatting, and long articles distorted some results, especially with
    character-weighted shares. The model provides an overview of this sample,
    not a measure of historical importance.

        # Assignment 5: Topic Modeling
    ## *The Boston Beacon*
    ## Genevieve Barbee GSB84

    **Question:** What kinds of writing make up this newspaper collection,
    and how do preprocessing choices and the number of topics change its apparent themes?

    Source line 73

        ## Part 1. Installation

    Source line 91

        ## Part 3a. Metadata-based predictions

    Given a newspaper published in Boston in the early 1880s, a reasonable initial
    expectation would be a mixture of local affairs, politics, commerce, and social
    announcements. Boston's urban setting suggests notices concerning streets,
    transportation, shops, and public institutions. Likely article varieties include
    reports of meetings, editorials, advertisements, marriage and death notices,
    church announcements, and some literary or entertaining material.

    Words such as *Boston, city, street, school, church, meeting, mayor, election,
    railroad, sale,* and *price* would therefore be plausible candidates for frequent
    use. National politics and commercial activity might seem likely to produce
    conspicuous topics. A further hypothesis is that fiction, poetry, or household
    advice will occur, but that the collection will be organized primarily around
    reporting public events.

    Source line 113

        ## Part 2. Select the newspaper

    Source line 166

        **Audit result:** The file contains **{expression} pages**. The selected
    English newspaper contributes **{expression} pages** across
    **{expression} distinct issue IDs**, from
    **{expression}** through **{expression}**.
    There are **{expression} excluded pages**.

    In this supplied file, the excluded records belong to `sn91058154`, are marked
    `yid`, and date to September 1919. Their text uses Hebrew script, although their
    title field also says *The Boston Beacon*. This is an identifier/language mismatch
    within the file, not evidence that one English newspaper suddenly changed language.
    Modeling only `sn91058053` avoids turning this mismatch into a misleading topic.

    Source line 184

        ## Prepare the text

    Full pages combine stories, notices, and advertisements, so I use smaller text
    segments as the model's documents. Paragraphs are packed into segments of about
    **300 whitespace-separated words**. Longer paragraphs are split; a short final
    fragment is attached to the previous segment, while other fragments below 60
    words are discarded. Segments with fewer than 40 retained alphabetic tokens are
    also discarded. No segment crosses a page boundary.

    Source line 244

        **Modeling corpus:** {expression} passages containing
    {expression} alphabetic tokens before stopword and frequency
    filtering. The median passage has {expression} such tokens.

    **Repetition:** {expression} passages
    ({expression}%) repeat an earlier passage's
    cleaned text exactly. They remain in the main models because this analysis
    characterizes the supplied collection as encountered. Repeated advertisements,
    recurring material, or duplicate content can therefore influence topic prevalence;
    these passages are not independent observations of historical events.

    Source line 271

        ## Part 3b. Train an LDA topic model

    Source line 334

        ## Part 5. Iterate: stopwords and topic counts

    The frequency list and initial model include honorifics such as *mrs, miss,* and
    *rev*. These help describe social conventions but can also fill topic summaries
    without saying much about their subject matter. The expanded list removes a
    small set of honorifics, abbreviated months, and publication terms. The table
    below makes that choice visible, including additions with no occurrences.

    I retain **Boston, Dorchester, street, church, Sunday, said**, and commercial
    vocabulary. Removing all common words would risk deleting the very local,
    religious, literary, or advertising patterns being investigated. For example,
    *said* can be useful evidence of dialogue, and *Sunday* can help identify church notices.

    The **10-topic baseline versus expanded-stoplist 10-topic model** isolates the
    stoplist change. The **6-, 10-, and 14-topic expanded-stoplist models** compare
    topic counts while holding the stoplist and other settings fixed.

    Source line 418

        ### What changed, and which model is most useful?

    The six-topic model offers a compact overview, but its broad groups combine
    multiple kinds of writing. The ten-topic model makes more distinctions, yet
    its word list containing *watch, notice, class,* and *license* combines watch
    advertising with official notices. At fourteen topics, liquor-license notices
    become a recognizable group, while health and patent-medicine language and
    household material can be inspected separately. For this exploratory reading,
    **I select the fourteen-topic model with the expanded stoplist**.

    More topics do not automatically mean a better model. In the fourteen-topic
    result, one topic still combines watch advertisements and yacht-club schedules,
    and several literary topics overlap. These limitations are visible in the
    source passages.

    Comparing the two ten-topic runs shows why the stoplist is an analytical choice:
    removing honorifics frees space in the summaries for other words and also changes
    how passages group together. It does not remove OCR errors or make every topic
    coherent. Words such as *Boston* and *street* remain deliberately visible because
    they help characterize local notices and commercial listings.

    Source line 468

        ## Part 4. Inspect topic words and highly weighted passages

    Source line 653

        ## Part 6. Discussion

    ### What the collection contains

    The strongest impression from the collection is its mixture of public information,
    neighborhood sociability, reading material, and practical advice. The local-notice
    topic brings together churches, schools, halls, and evening events. Its highly
    weighted passages include an installation of officers of a Sons of Veterans camp
    and a succession of neighborhood announcements. These texts support the expectation
    of local affairs, but they also show that local affairs meant a social and institutional
    calendar, not simply municipal politics.

    The literary material is particularly interesting. The model separates dialogue and
    family stories, sentimental or poetic descriptions, and a group of passages involving
    recurring characters such as Milly and Barbara. The latter is a reminder that a topic
    can follow the vocabulary of a particular serialized narrative rather than describe a
    broad historical subject. The passage beginning *Song of the Rain* supports the poetry
    interpretation, while the story involving Judge Ford and the missing child shows why
    recurring character names become informative to the model.

    Other groups reveal the newspaper's practical uses. Recipes and washing instructions
    support the household label. Railroad and steamship schedules explain combinations
    such as *leave, return, trains,* and *station*. The liquor-license passages repeatedly
    state that applicants have requested permission to sell intoxicating liquors. Here,
    formulaic language produces an unusually distinct topic. These examples are much more
    specific than the general expectation that a newspaper would contain advertisements
    and official notices.

    ### Expectations and surprises

    The predicted local, religious, commercial, and transportation content is present.
    However, it seems  the hypothesis that reporting public events would dominate the organization
    of the collection was too narrow. Fiction, verse, advice, and recurring notices are
    central to the model's overview. The page-position heatmap helps explain this pattern:
    local announcements are especially associated with page 2, while dialogue and serial
    fiction are more prominent on pages 1 and 4. Schedules and some advertisements appear
    strongly on page 3. These are tendencies within the supplied passages, not rigid rules
    about every issue; pages 5 and 6 are represented much less often.

    What surprised me is that words suggestive of a subject do not
    establish the genre of a passage. The health topic's strongest examples are
    testimonials promoting Renne's Magic Oil and other remedies. They are evidence of
    historical advertising, not evidence that the advertised treatment worked. Similarly,
    a passage with the language of politics and literary criticism turns out to be an
    advertisement for *The Nation*. The keyword list alone could easily encourage a reader
    to overstate the amount of political reporting in that topic.

    ### How the techniques helped and hindered interpretation

    Topic modeling helps by directing attention to recurrent combinations of words across
    15,940 passages. It makes an otherwise very large collection approachable and points
    to material that can then be read closely. Allowing mixtures also matters: an
    advertisement, a story, and a religious reflection can share vocabulary without being
    the same kind of text. SQL rankings make the interpretation inspectable by preserving
    source identifiers and showing both word probabilities and actual passages.

    At the same time, the results expose the limitations of the input and method. One
    highly weighted passage in the religion/verse topic consists largely of the phrase
    *not so soft* repeated many times. This appears to be corruption or repetition in the
    supplied OCR text; it should not be interpreted as a meaningful historical theme.
    Repetition also operates at a larger scale: 2,349 passages repeat earlier cleaned text
    exactly, before considering near-duplicates. Repeated advertisements and variations
    between editions can therefore shape topics disproportionately.

    The model ignores word order, so it does not recognize argument, irony, or a sales
    pitch on its own. Short alphabetic tokenization removes dates, prices, and some names;
    it also leaves OCR mistakes and fragments. Approximate segmentation can mix neighboring
    items and split a single story. Removing honorifics makes some summaries clearer but
    also removes socially meaningful information.

    I would consequently use this model as a guide to further reading, not a definitive
    taxonomy of the newspaper or a measure of what its readers cared about.

        **How to read these diagnostics:** Top-word uniqueness describes repetition
    across word lists; a larger value is not proof of better topics. The largest
    topic weight describes concentration, not prediction accuracy. Topic counts
    themselves affect both measures. Model choice below also uses the actual words
    and highly weighted passages. This is exploratory analysis, without a held-out
    evaluation or a claim to have found an optimal number of topics.

    passages show why even a highly weighted example can be an OCR artifact.

      Source line 648

          **What was in the collection: **

    **Politics, elections and slavery:** There were topics that included whig, convention, election, government, and constitution. AN example of this was a letter in August 16, 1856 from Goodrich to Toombs to discuss Congress Power to restrict slavery in the territories

    **Legislative Proceedings:** Words contained bill, committee, house, senate, motion, and passed

    **Shipping and whaling:** Port arrivals, departures, vessel locations, and “WHALERS” reports. The 30-topic cleaned model separates a topic containing bedford, ship, oil, and bbls.

    **Markets and business:** Commodity prices, stock sales, company notices, insolvency notices, and business partnerships.

    **Everyday life:** Marriage and death notices, transport schedules, fires, and court reporting.

    **Foreign affairs:** Topics containing french, russian, british, and war. Article examples discuss European news and the Russia-Turkey dispute.

    For example, in the cleaned 10 topic run, a December 14, 1853 notice about trains to New York through Worcester and Springfield, and a June 29, 1853 notice about travel through Fall River and Newport, both appeared in K-means cluster 5. Both also had LDA topic 7 as their strongest topic, with weights of about 93%. That topic included words like “boston,” “railroad,” and “o’clock.” Reading the notices showed why the methods agreed: both described transportation routes and departure schedules.

    **How did this compare with my prediction: **

    My prediction about politics and slavery were supported by actual articles, not just topic words.
    I also found discussion of the Mexican-American War and territorial expansion. For example, “TRUTH FROM THE RIGHT QUARTER,” July 27, 1850, connects Texas annexation, the Mexican War, and disputes over newly acquired territories.
    There is a limitation: only 92 extracted articles date from 1846–1848, and none from 1847. About 84% of the articles date from 1851–1856. This means the date range was misleading in the number of articles that were covered.

    **What changed after cleaning the stoplist:**
    So I had added more words to the stoplist since some of the original topics included words like td, tr, vt, ventral. After running 10,20,30 topic models on this new stop list, the new topic model changed from: td tr table rr vt bank boston tbody central board to boston railroad bank company stock central board rr vt kind. Even though this didn’t fix all of the issues, it helped some interpretation. This is also once case, topics change across runs due to

    **What changed with the number of topics:**
    10 topics: useful broad overview, but transportation and different business notices were grouped together.
    20 topics: clearer separation of elections, legislation, local news, markets, and foreign affairs.
    30 topics: additional detail, including whaling and narrower shipping categories, but also more topics dominated by names or vague words.

    More topics did not automatically mean better.

    **What did K means add**
    In the cleaned 10 topic run, separate clusters for travel schedules, insolvency notices, and business partnerships all aligned most strongly with LDA topic 7. This shows how K-means separated article types that LDA combined into a broader theme.

    Some groups, like market reports and personal notices, matched the LDA topics pretty closely. But K-means put travel schedules, insolvency notices, and company notices into separate groups, while the 10-topic LDA model mostly grouped them under one broader business topic. Showed me that the two methods could organize the same articles differently, and neither was necessarily right or wrong.

    What surprised me most was how much routine information appeared, such as shipping reports, market prices, and personal notices. The uneven coverage across years also surprised me. Topic words helped me identify possible themes, while reading example articles helped me check those interpretations.

            # Part 3: Train a topic model

    I use `LatentDirichletAllocation` from `pyrmallet` to model the newspaper
    as a mixture of topics. Before training, each OCR page is divided into
    article-sized documents. This gives the model shorter, more meaningful
    units than treating an entire newspaper issue as one document.

        Source line 50

            ## Predictions before examining the data

    Since this collection is from Boston and approximately 1908–1911, I
    expect Progressive Era reform, trust-busting, labor, immigration,
    socialism, national politics, foreign affairs, and local Massachusetts
    news to appear. I also expect political reports, editorials, crime and
    court reports, business news, advertisements, notices, and sports.

    Likely common words include `city`, `state`, `government`, `president`,
    `people`, `year`, `said`, `Roosevelt`, and `Taft`. OCR errors and newspaper
    layout artifacts may also produce frequent terms that are not meaningful
    topics.

        Source line 108

            ## 1. Load and segment the OCR

    **{expression}** contains **{expression}** pages from
    **{expression}** through **{expression}**.
    I treat lines beginning with `#` as article boundaries, then merge short
    segments so that headlines and fragments remain with nearby article text.

        Source line 182

            The segmentation produced **{expression}** article documents from
    **{expression}** pages. The minimum length control reduces the chance
    that a headline or OCR fragment becomes an entire document by itself.

        Source line 192

            ## 2. Configure the LDA model

    The stoplist removes common function words that are unlikely to distinguish
    topics. The main model uses 25 topics. Training
    always uses 200 Gibbs-sampling iterations and a minimum document frequency of
    5, so all three models use the same training settings.

        Source line 272

                ### Training complete

        The model learned **{expression}** topics from
        **{expression}** vocabulary words after removing
        **{expression}** stopwords. Each article now has a probability
        distribution over the learned topics.

            ## Discussion

        Source line 496

            The findings in the collection are very different from what I expected, and some parts are confusing. The most prominent topic is topic 19 with "great, may, years, people, men, fact, every, good, work, business, even, world". I assume this is talking about how great the economy is doing. It is also pretty interesting to see the sharp decline of topic 22, "man, know, think, go, say, good, day, never, us, take, money, right" over the years, while topic 0 "state, year, bill, new, house, governor, committee, commission, senator, public, law, massachusetts" increases steadily. Because it is closer and closer to the election.

        Source line 504

            Across the 3 models, I see some pretty weird characters such as "td, tr, th". I initially thought those were initials, but based on the topics they are in, that seems unlikely. They could just be OCR transcription errors. Also, I see a lot of mentions of New York and Chicago across different topics and different runs, which is an interesting finding.

        Source line 512

            Also, when comparing topics, you can see that the 50-topic run has so many topics that some feel like they could be combined while still retaining some distinction. On the other hand, the 5-topic model produces very high-level topics, as expected. However, even within those 5 topics, we can still see the "td, tr, th" group.

        Source line 520

            For this dataset, 25 topics are enough, and 50 topics are too much information; it's hard to name the topics or distinguish them from one another, and 5 topics are incoherent.

            #Analysis
    In my first iteration, I tested 30 topics and 150 iterations. Findings were definitely quite different from my initial predictions. I think the biggest surprise that came to me was that there was a much wider variety of topics than I thought. It was not just traditional news reports, but also included things like recipes, sports, clothes, and such. While my predicted vocabulary and topics did exist in some of the found categories, they weren't at the top.
    Top 2 results were actually completely unexpected. The first was words like "time told mother asked see room back know face saw yes go little away house knew looked even heard door" and the second was "man think says know world never young good exercise go say girl love things men see way want tell every". These both were a bit hard to predict, and the first one seems to hint at more narrative writing, while the second might be more related to either romance or maybe a advice column. The third top topic was a lot more in line with my prediction, regarding more politics with words like "president, union, committee, etc". There was also financial topics like I predicted (topic 21: company, stock, business, trust, money, etc.) Some smaller grouped topics I didn't predict include crime reports (topic 16: police, found, hospital), sports (topic 13: game, team, ball, club, score), and domestic life/women (topic 18: life, women, man, children, home)

    I then tried both increasing the number of topics seeing that they were more diverse than I thought, and some categories seemed to have vocabulary from two different topics merged together. I also tried increasing the number of iterations to see if it improves the results. What I noticed was that 50 topics might've been too much, but it did separate out a few topics that were confusing earlier in the 30 topic iteration, but makes more sense now. For example, topic 0 in the 30 topic run had "time class cup water race three horses milk add heat put sugar one-half races cook horse half salt five hot". However with 50 topics, it more cleanly split into a recipe focused topic and horse racing topic (topic 26: water cup milk put add three sugar etc, topic 40: race class time second won track races third mile club). Some topics also became clearly less noisy, for example I was more confused by what topic 20 could be in the 30 topic run (white black blue hair red hat little long green book women used sir back light large front crowd followed met), while it became more clearly related to fashion in the 50 topic and more iteration run (white blue black red green hat inches wide little long low shape women lace dress wear color gown worn large). I belive that number of components helped separate out some collaprsed/grouped topics from 30 to 50, while increasing the number of iterations helped remove some more noisy/seemingly random top words in some of the topics.

            ##Predictions

    This dataset comes from a newspaper published in Springfield, Massachusetts, focusing on the period between 1892-1896. Springfield is known for their manufacturing sector back then, producing the first American gasoline powered car in 1893. They were known for their automobiles and trolley lines, which allowed workers to commute long distances. I predict there will be themes revolving around labor and wages, maybe worker protests, and new inventions. There will still be areas of public life being discussed, like maybe a town hall or important meetings announcements. I expect there to be topics on just the culture of the time, like popular fashion trends and new forms of entertainment. Big events were probably published in the newspaper as well, like crime and social gatherings.

            ##Findings

    My original findings, using the blueprint from the example code, don't differ so much from I predicted. Granted, I don't know too much about American history in the 1890s, even less so for a localized area such as Springfield, but I have a general idea of how the culture and time period is different from modern customs. There were topics related to fashion, sailing, war, crime, and work. I was curious about some of top words for each topic; for example, in the third topic that revolves around the theme of fashion, the top snippets are all about fashion tips and advice. However, the top words include "screamed, idiot, little, whiskered" which...doesn't make any sense? And these words ranked higher compared to words like "lace, pretty, color" which make more sense in the context of clothing.

    So I ran a SQL query through the documents in the topic that contains words like "idiot," and found out that the documents with those words have a low probability within the topic itself (so they're not really about fashion). Yet why would the word "idiot" be ranked within the top 10 words for that topic then? I looked into the document with the highest probability that includes this word (the document focuses on observations at a convention, with some remakrs about the clothing of the people there), and turns out the text itself has an error that completely skewed the top words - one of the sentences talks about a man screaming "you whiskered idiot" over and over again, and that's why it was labeled as one of the top words. I think it might be an OCR parsing error, or perhaps the one writing the article had a vendetta.

    Some of the topics were too vague for me to decipher based on the words alone, and after looking through some of the documents I thought it would make more sense to increase the number of topics to see if clearer categories can be defined. I then ran the model on 50 topics and see if there is a better distinction. I think there were more subcategories that make sense (i.e. one topic that seems to focus on chinese and japanese immigrants), but the model definitely overfitted some of the words. Topic 11, for example, grouped documents that had several hyphenated words at the end of the line (aka words that can't fit on one line so they get hyphenated to start on the next line).

    One topic that remains the consistently largest percentage of the corpus deals with articles on advice. Looking into some of the texts, these "advice columns" revolve around women and cleaning, and men and money. The top documents talks about using a new type of lard for cleaning and tips for housekeeping (both written for a female audience). The articles that focuses on men discusses effective business strategies and saving/earning money.

                # Assignment 5: Topic Modeling

                ## Part 3: Predictions (written before looking at the data)

    The selected file is a Massachusetts newspaper from the 19th century.
    Before loading anything, here is what I expect to find.

    **Themes**

    - **Politics.** State legislature and town affairs; national politics
      (Congress, presidential campaigns, tariffs). Depending on the decade:
      slavery and abolition, the Mexican War, the Civil War and
      Reconstruction, and later labor/monetary questions. Massachusetts was a
      Whig-then-Republican stronghold with an active reform press, so
      abolition, temperance, and religion should be prominent.
    - **Commerce and shipping.** Boston was a major port. Ship arrivals and
      departures, cargo, "prices current" for goods (flour, sugar, cotton,
      wool, molasses), bank and insurance notices.
    - **Agriculture and rural life** if the paper is outside Boston: crops,
      livestock, weather, county fairs.
    - **Religion and moral reform.** Sermons, church news, temperance
      societies, Sabbath schools.
    - **Railroads** (timetables, construction, accidents), and later
      telegraph news.
    - **Foreign news** arriving "by the steamer": England, France, Ireland
      (famine and emigration), Continental revolutions.

    **Varieties of article**

    - Advertisements will dominate by volume: dry goods, patent medicines,
      real-estate and auction notices, wanted/lost notices, legal and probate
      notices (highly formulaic language).
    - Marriage and death notices; obituaries.
    - Serialized fiction, poetry, moral tales, anecdotes and jokes.
    - Editorials and letters "To the Editor".
    - Short local items ("Fire in Main Street", "The Fourth of July").
    - Official proceedings: town meetings, court sessions, legislative
      reports.

    **Words I expect to be common** (after removing standard stopwords)

    `mr`, `mrs`, `said`, `boston`, `street`, `county`, `town`, `day`,
    `house`, `land`, `sale`, `sold`, `dollars`, `cents`, `court`, `church`,
    `state`, `government`, `war`, `ship`, `bark`, `schooner`, `wool`,
    `cotton`, `flour`, `died`, `married`, `aged`, `years`, `will`, `may`.

    **Predictions about the model itself**

    - At least one topic will be pure OCR garbage (`tho`, `tbe`, `aud`,
      `ot`, `iu`, short nonsense strings), and one or more will be
      dominated by numbers/prices before I filter them out.
    - Advertising and legal-notice topics will be the most coherent because
      their language is formulaic and repeated.
    - Topics will often correspond to *genre* (ads, shipping lists, poetry)
      rather than *subject*, because genre vocabulary is more consistent
      than subject vocabulary.
    - Increasing k will split advertising into finer product categories
      before it splits the news into finer subjects.

            Source line 197

                ## Load the file and split pages into articles

    The BPL dataset stores one *page* per line, with OCR text broken into
    segmented "crops" (columns/blocks) already sorted in reading order and
    labelled by type (e.g. `Content`, `Section heading`). A new article is
    started at each heading-type crop; image crops are dropped. If a file
    instead has a single `text` field per page, a fallback splits on blank
    lines that precede an ALL-CAPS line.

            Source line 324

                    **Unboxing:** {expression} pages → {expression} documents
        ({expression} tokens) in {expression}s.

        Fields in a page record: `{expression}`

            Source line 386

                ## Stoplist

    A standard English stoplist plus a first round of newspaper-specific
    additions (honorifics, generic time words) and a few frequent OCR
    misreadings (`tho` = the, `tbe` = the, `aud` = and, `ot` = of). Tokens are
    already restricted to alphabetic strings of ≥3 letters, which removes
    numbers, prices, and most one/two-letter OCR debris.

            Source line 424

                ## Part 3 (bis): Train a topic model

            Source line 464

            Trained k={expression} on {expression} docs, vocabulary {expression} types, in {expression}s.

            Source line 473

                ## Part 4: Analyze with SQL

    Three tables in DuckDB: `topic_words` (top 100 words per topic),
    `doc_topics` (document × topic weights), and `docs` (metadata + snippet).

            Source line 629

                ## Part 5: Iterate

    Two kinds of iteration: (a) find "promiscuous" words that show up in the
    top words of many topics and add them to the stoplist, then retrain;
    (b) compare different values of k on a subsample.

            Source line 761

                ## Part 6: Discussion

    **What did you find?** The file was *The Boston American*, a Hearst
    daily from roughly 1908–1911. At k=25 the corpus is dominated by sports
    (baseball, Harvard–Yale, and a boxing topic that is essentially the
    1910 Johnson–Jeffries fight), crime and courts, city/state politics,
    society columns, foreign news, the stock market, women's-page material
    (fashion, household and health advice), theatre, automobiles, real
    estate notices, and serialized fiction.

    **Predictions vs. findings.** My main miss was the period: I predicted
    a 19th-century paper, so shipping lists, agriculture, abolition, and
    temperance never appeared, and instead I got the 20th-century
    mass-circulation mix of sports, crime, society, and automobiles. The
    structural predictions held: topics track genre more than subject,
    formulaic text (real estate, club notices, society columns) gave the
    cleanest topics, and `boston`/`york`/`street`/`said` were the expected
    high-frequency filler.

    **Most surprising.** One event, the Johnson–Jeffries fight, owned a
    top-five topic across four years. `tbody` showed up as a top baseball
    word because the OCR emits box scores as HTML tables, and the top
    documents for the household-advice topic all began "Copyright, 1910,"
    revealing syndicated boilerplate the word list alone would never have
    shown.

    **How the techniques helped or hindered.** Alphabetic-only
    tokenization removed numbers but let artifacts like `tbody` and `sed`
    through, and the article splitter mostly failed on this file (a single
    `text` field per page), so most documents are 500-word page chunks
    rather than articles. Removing the five promiscuous words (`boston`,
    `york`, `house`, `made`, `street`) noticeably sharpened the topics, the
    2D map made near-duplicate topics obvious, and reading top documents
    rather than word lists is what exposed both the syndication and the
    chunking problems.

                # Assignment 5: Topic Modeling

    ## Newspaper Collection

    **Newspaper:** The Gloucester Telegraph
    **Location:** Gloucester, Massachusetts
    **Date range:** 1858–1867
    **Number of pages:** 2,722

            Source line 35

                ## Predictions

    Before examining the newspaper text, I expect to find themes related to:

    - shipping, fishing, and maritime activity
    - local businesses and commerce
    - the Civil War and national political events
    - weather and events affecting the Gloucester community

    ### Expected types of articles

    - local news
    - advertisements and business notices
    - shipping or port reports
    - political and war-related news
    - legal or public notices
    - births, deaths, and community announcements

    ### Words I expect to appear often

    - ship
    - harbor
    - fish
    - Gloucester
    - Boston
    - war
    - army
    - sale
    - captain

    These predictions are based only on the newspaper's location and date range, before examining the OCR text.

            Source line 389

                ### Topic 9 Interpretation

    Topic 9 looks like it represents Civil War and military-related news.

    The top words for this topic included terms like war, army, regiment,
    government, and rebel. Looking at the highly weighted documents helped
    confirm this interpretation. One of the strongest documents discussed the
    United States Army, Fort Sumter, Fort Pickens, the War Department, military
    weapons, and Jefferson Davis.

    This shows why examining the documents themselves is useful rather than
    looking only at the topic's highest-weighted words.

            Source line 420

                ### Topic 5 Interpretation

    Topic 5 seems to represent commercial advertisements and product notices,
    including medicines and household products.

    The top words initially suggested a health-related topic because words like
    *colds*, *coughs*, and *health* appeared a lot. However, examining the
    highly weighted documents showed that the topic was broader than just medicine alone. The documents included advertisements for products such as Ayer's Cherry Pectoral and concentrated leaven, as well as business and
    copartnership notices.

    This shows that the highest-weighted words alone can sometimes give an
    incomplete impression of a topic, and that examining the documents heps ensure the interpretation is more accurate.

            Source line 452

                ### Topic 2 Interpretation

    Topic 2 looks like it represents community announcements, especially marriages and deaths.

    The top words included terms like yrs, Mrs., Miss, county, and place names such as Essex. At first, words such as estate and court made the topic seem like it might focus on legal notices. However, the highly weighted documents showed many marriage and death announcements containing names, ages, and towns.

    This topic seems to capture local community records and personal announcements more broadly

            Source line 534

                ## Iteration

    I first trained a topic model with 10 topics. This model produced several
    interpretable patterns, including Civil War and military news, advertisements, and local community announcements.

    The first model also had some unhelpful OCR fragments and abbreviations,
    such as th, sch, glou, td, and tr (which I had to look up because I was not sure what those were). I added these terms to the stopword list along with standard English stopwords.

    I then trained a second model with 15 topics and 50 iterations. The larger
    number of topics divided the newspaper collection into more specific groups.
    For example, a military topic remained clearly visible with words like war, army, regiment, government, and rebel.

    Comparing the two models showed that both the number of topics and the choice of stopwords affect how the collection is organized and how easily the topics can be interpreted.

            Source line 554

                # Discussion

    Before examining the collection, I predicted that The Gloucester Telegraph
    would include themes having to do with ships and maritime activity, local business, the Civil War, community events, and advertisements.

    The topic model supported several of these predictions. One of the
    clearest topics involved Civil War and military news. Its highest-weighted
    words included terms like war, army, regiment, government, and rebel. Highly weighted documents discussed the United States Army, Fort
    Sumter, Fort Pickens, the War Department, and Jefferson Davis.

    Another topic involved commercial advertisements and product notices. At
    first, words like colds, coughs, and health made this topic seem mostly related to medicine. However, examining the highly weighted documents
    showed advertisements for products such as Ayer's Cherry Pectoral and
    concentrated leaven, along with business notices.

    A third topic looked at local community announcements, especially marriages
    and deaths. Highly weighted documents contained names, ages, towns, marriage
    announcements, and death notices.

    One of the most surprising parts of the analysis was the amount of OCR noise
    in the collection. Some topic words were fragments or abbreviations rather
    than meaningful themes. Adding some of these terms to the stopword list helped reduce this problem.

    Topic modeling was useful because it provided an overview of thousands of
    newspaper pages that would be difficult to read individually. However, it also had limitations. A single newspaper page can contain multiple unrelated
    articles, advertisements, and notices, so one document can contain several
    different subjects. OCR errors also made some topics more difficult to
    interpret.

    Overall, looking at both the highest-weighted words and the highest-weighted
    documents was more useful than relying on topic words alone. The results
    supported many of my original predictions while also showing that some
    topics were broader and more complicated than I initially thought they would be.

                # Assignment 5: Topic Modeling — *The Worcester Spy* (1898–1904)

    ## Part 3: Predictions (written befoire looking at the data)

    **Context:** a small industrial New England city, 1898–1904: the Spanish-American War, McKinley
    (assassinated 1901), Theodore Roosevelt, the Boer War, streetcars and bicycles.

    **Themes/words we expect**:

    1. **War and empire** — *war, army, troops, general, Cuba, Manila, Spain, Filipinos*
    2. **National politics** — *president, republican, democrat, election, congress, senate, bill*
    3. **Local Worcester news** — *city, council, police, court, fire, street, committee*
    4. **Advertising**  *sale, price, cents, cure, ladies, cloth*
    5. **Society / social notices** — *church, Mrs., Miss, married, died, funeral, club, lodge*
    6. **Sports** — *game, team, inning, won, score, race, Yale, Harvard*
    7. **Business / finance** — *stock, market, bonds, railroad, wool*
    8. **Legal notices** — *estate, deceased, probate, court, hereby*

    **Typical article varieties:** short local paragraphs, telegraph wire dispatches ("Washington, Nov. 3"),
    classified ads, legal notices, market tables, sports box scores, serialized fiction / household columns.

    **Problems we expect:** OCR errors with identifying words, a topic that is
    only OCR noise or ad boilerplate, and some topics that blend war news with politics.

    **Least sure about:** a clean sports topic; and that *Worcester* will be a top word in many topics, so it may need to go on the stoplist.

            Source line 66

                ## Part 2: Load the newspaper and split pages into articles

    The file has one page per line and each page's text is markdown. For example, an article starts at a
    `# Heading` line. We split at those headings and keep the heading plus the text under it as one
    "document". Many chunks are just a heading, so we drop
    chunks shorter than 400 characters. We also drop 104 pages from 1886 that belong to a
    different paper ID (`sn89062065`).

            Source line 135

                ## Part 3: Train a first topic model (k = 20, standard English stoplist)

            Source line 215

                ## Part 5: Iterate

    The first model shows two problems we predicted: an **OCR/markup junk topic** (`td tr tbody thead`
    — HTML table tags left in the text) and **very generic words** (`said`, `new`, `mr`, `mrs`, `st`,
    `worcester`, first names) that show up in many topics. We add these to the stoplist and compare
    **k = 10, 20, 40**.

            Source line 290

                ## Topics over time and across the page

    With the k=40 model we can ask *when* topics appear (mean topic weight by year) and *where on the
    page* (page number). We chart the topics whose weight changes the most across years.

            Source line 345

                ## Part 6: Discussion
    (Edited/Polished with Claude)

    **What we found.** After splitting ~18.8k pages into ~191k articles with 20k sampled per model, the
    topics that came out cleanly and obviously were **sports** (baseball box scores and league standings, college
    football, racing and track with the k=40 model seperting *box scores* from *team/game prose* from
    *races*, **church and society** notices, **funerals/deaths**, **police and court** news,
    **legal notices** (estates, probate, mortgagee sales), **stock-market reports**, **railroad
    timetables**, **city council/ordinances/committees**, **party politics and elections**, **war and
    military news** (*gen war army british*), **theatre**, **fashion**, and **fiction/essays** (*life
    people world love*). Where the topics show up on the page is very clear: sports is concentrated on
    **page 3**, national/war politics on **page 1**, church news on **page 5**, and essays/fiction
    on **page 7**.

    **Compared with our predictions.** Most predicted themes appeared such as war, politics, local news,
    society, sports, finance, legal notices. Our predicted OCR-junk topic also appeared in the first
    pass, one topic was entirely HTML table tags (`td tr tbody thead`). What did *not* match was that we
    predicted **advertising** would be a big, clean topic, but no topic is clearly "ads" — ads are
    spread into fashion, finance and the other topics. We expected *Worcester* to dominate many topics;
    it did show up widely, so we stopped it. We expected war and politics to blur — they did stay
    partly separate (topic 15 is war/army; topic 13 is elections), though "president/government"
    words link them.

    **Most surprising.** (1) How much of the paper is **structured data, not prose**: box scores, stock
    tables, train timetables, and legal notices each earn their *own* topics, because they use very
    distinctive vocabulary. (2) The **strong page layout** — the model recovers the paper's sections
    without ever being told page numbers. (3) The article on President McKinley's shooting ("Buffalo, Sept. 10 ... bulletin which all the doctors signed", 1901) is assigned 0.99 to k=40 topic 37 (*town removed slight returned webster disturbance*) — a topic that mixes the bulletin's medical wording (*removed, slight, disturbance*) with small-town news (*Webster, Spencer*). The model groups by *words*, not by *event*, so a famous national event has no topic of its own. (4) A few topics we could not interpret — e.g. k=40 topic 12 (*nov gray oct
    liberty clinton dec caroline eva*) and the recurring *altogether / appreciated / drawing waiting*
    words, which look like repeated boilerplate or OCR quirks rather than a theme. We did not
    investigate these further.

    **Effect of iterating.** Adding a stoplist of markup tags, titles (*mr, mrs*), *said*, and common
    first names removed the junk topic and made topics less generic. **k=10** gives broad sections
    (sports / politics-war / church / court / markets), **k=20** is a good overview, and **k=40**
    splits sports and legal notices into sub-types but also produces some fragmented or
    name-list topics. There is no single "right" k; it depends on the question.

    **How the methods helped or hindered.** *Helped:* LDA gives a fast "unboxing" of 190k articles no
    one could read; looking at the **top words and the top article for each topic together** (via SQL)
    was much more reliable than words alone; the by-year and by-page views turned topics into
    findings. *Hindered:* topic labels are our interpretation; OCR errors and tables produce noise;
    each topic's top words can look coherent while its documents are mixed; results depend on the
    stoplist, k, and the random sample; and the by-year chart is noisy (1898 and 1904 are partial years,
    and the "movers" list is dominated by topics whose share is small). The war topic peaks in 1898 and
    1900 and rises again in 1904, which fits the Spanish-American War and later conflicts, but we did
    not check individual articles to confirm that.

                # Newspaper topic model

    1. Choose a newspaper file, load its OCR pages and and split them into articles at lines starting with `#`.
    2. Merge segments that are too short.
    3. Edit the stoplist, pick a number of topics, and train with `pyrmallet`.
    4. Query the resulting `topics`, `word_topics`, `documents` and `doc_topics` tables with SQL.

                  ##Part 5

              Source line 430

                  ####**Base model setup:**
    - I fixed the random seed at 18 so that differences between runs come from the parameters I changed (number of topics, stoplist, iterations) rather than from pure randomness.
    - Base parameters: I chose 20 topics, 250 iterations, a minimum article length of 1,000 characters, and a minimum document frequency of 7. I chose 20 topics as a middle ground so that unrelated subjects weren't forced together, but not so many that topics become too granular to interpret. I kept iterations at 250 because each training run took about 15 minutes (and more for other experiments). The 1,000 character minimum keeps articles from being too fragmented.
    - Before training, I removed articles with fewer than 50 real words. Without this, the model silently dropped 32 near-empty OCR fragments, which seemed to misalign the topic proportions with the article table (I kept getting an error message)
    - The results table above shows this base run, which are discussed further in part 6.

    ####**Experiment 1: Number of topics**
    - 40 topics: Topics became much more specific and better differentiated. For example, the 20-topic model had sports split mainly into boxing, baseball, and school/college athletics, while the 40-topic model separated college and professional sports more cleanly. Some topics became too narrow to interpret, such as "country professor dr art england old years latest say famous."
    - 10 topics: Topics blurred together in ways the larger models showed should be separate. For example, one topic combined "miss, theatre, opera, king, london," mixing entertainment with foreign news. 10 topics is too few for this corpus.
    - Based on these, approx. 30 topics would likely balance specificity and interpretability.

    ####**Experiment 2: Adding to the stoplist**
    - Added: td, tr, th, boston, upon, shall, may, must. td, tr, and th may be HTML table tags left in the OCR output (see Part 6), and are probably not real words that contribute to the text. "boston" appears across nearly every kind of article in a Boston paper, so it does not help distinguish topics. The others are filler words missing from the default list.
    - Result: several previously vague topics became clearer. For example, the advertisment category became more clear

    ####**Experiment 3: Increasing iterations from 250 to 500**
    - With everything else at the base settings, produced no clearly noticeable change in the topics. This suggests the model had largely converged by 250 iterations.

              Source line 454

                  ##Part 6

              Source line 462

                  ####**What I found**
    - The biggest takeaway is that the two largest topics (19 and 5, about 12% each) are not really "news". Their top words are things like "man, little, know, love, woman" and "great, work, people, life," which suggests fiction, serialized stories, and opinion columns make up a large share of the paper.
    - Beyond that, the paper is dominated by local content: city politics, courts and crime, fires, church and club meetings, and society news. Sports also take up a large share, split across boxing, baseball, and college athletics.
    - Smaller topics cover business and the stock market, real estate, foreign news, automobiles, theater, fashion, and classified ads.

    ####**Comparison to my predictions**
    Note: I incorrectly predicted based on the late 19th century because (at first) I read the index entry for a different file by accident. The file I actually loaded covers the early 20th century, which explains these differences from my predictions.
    - Similar to what I expected, there was a defined technology topic, but it was about automobiles rather than railroads or the telegraph, which makes sense given the real dates
    - My prediction about personal life announcements was largely right, with society news, church events, and court cases all showing up as topics.
    - Most surprising: I expected advertisements to be a major theme, but it was smaller compared to the themes mentioned previously

    Additional thoughts:
    - Other techniques that could help include cleaning out HTML/typos before training, comparing LDA with clustering like k-means, or using word embeddings to group together OCR misspellings that LDA treats as separate words.

                  #**Part 3: Makes Predications**

              Source line 43

                  I selected The Boston Beacon, with 1,475 pages, and the time period is from 1830 to 1881. I believe the themes present will be heavy on social reform and industrial revolution, since Boston was going through a lot of change during that era (additionally, the abolitionist movement). Manifest Destiny was also in 1845 which was a big moment in American history so there would likely be a lot of key words related to westward expansion and important global events. Typical varieties of articles could be current news focus, international happenings, local events taking place, sports, business/industrial updates, fashion movements, socialist movements, ongoing trends, etc. Words that I expect to be common are, activism, social reform, expansion, westward, immigration, historic, economy, abolition, slavery, union, war, Whig, Irish, famine, panic, bank, California, gold, textile, silk, etc.

                  What did you find in the collection? Compare your actual findings to your guesses. What was most surprising? Describe how the text mining techniques you applied helped (or hindered) you in finding and understanding patterns.

    The topic model revealed themes that I didn't predict. A large share of topics were serialized fiction, poetry, and romantic narrative rather than news coverage, and religion (church activities, sermons, faith-based language) appeared far more prominently than expected. Predicted themes like abolition and slavery weren't strongly represented among the top topics, while local neighborhood notices (real estate, cemeteries, street names) and what appears to be Civil War-era enlistment language did emerge, aligning partially with my expansion/reform predictions but pointing to a later period that I did not focus on. The text mining approach was useful for surfacing broad thematic clusters at scale, but the OCR quality (stray HTML tags like "td"/"tr" appearing as a topic) and short, generic filler words in some topics made a few clusters hard to interpret without reading actual article snippets alongside the word lists. These were my takeaways for a model trained on 50 topics.

    Dropping to 30 topics didn't reduce the fiction/generic-filler noise. Those themes persisted as strongly as at 50 topics. The more specific topics (crime, railroads/finance, local real estate) stayed consistent across both runs.

                    ## Newspaper selection and predictions

    I chose the *Western Hampden Times*, which was published in Westfield, Massachusetts. The available issues range from 1869 to 1875.

    Before looking at the actual newspaper text, I expect the collection to include a mix of local Westfield news and larger national events. Since this period falls shortly after the Civil War, I would expect to see discussion of Reconstruction, elections, President Grant, and other political issues. I also think there will be topics related to railroads, local businesses, agriculture, churches, schools, and economic issues like the Panic of 1873, which I did admittedly have to Google.

    The newspaper will probably contain several different types of writing, including political reporting, local announcements, advertisements, obituaries, business notices, and possibly serialized stories. Some words I expect to appear often are *Westfield*, *Massachusetts*, *town*, *county*, *election*, *railroad*, *market*, *church*, *school*, *sale*, and *dollars*. Since this is historical OCR data, I also expect some words to be misspelled, misread, or awkwardly split up during the scanning process.

                Source line 331

                    ### Initial 10-topic model

    The first model produced several fairly recognizable topics. Topic 0 focused on like local court cases, estates, and legal notices, while Topic 7 contained political terms such as *state*, *Republican*, *party*, and *president*. Topic 8 was clearly related to railroad schedules and regional travel, and Topic 9 focused on local churches, schools, and community events. The model also seemed to separate sentimental or religious writing in Topic 2 from more narrative or fictional writing in Topic 4. Topic 6 was probably home remedies or health advertisements based on words such as *vinegar*, *water*, and *bitters*.

    Topic 3 was mostly made up of table-formatting terms such as *td*, *tr*, *table*, and *tbody*, which came from the document formatting rather than the newspaper’s actual content. Topic 5 was also less coherent than the others and appeared to mix local announcements with agricultural terms. Based on these results, I added the obvious formatting artifacts to the stoplist and increased the model from 10 to 15 topics to see whether some of the broader or mixed topics would separate more clearly.

                Source line 539

                    ## Refined model and topic labels

    After looking at the first model, I added the formatting and table-related words to the stopword list and increased the number of topics from 10 to 15. The refined model used 211 stopwords and produced topics that were easier to interpret i believe.

    | Topic | My interpretation                                      |
    | ----- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
    | 0     | agriculture, animal feed, and crops in units           |
    | 1     | religious and moral writing, a lot about mothers       |
    | 2     | fiction and goofy domestic stories                     |
    | 3     | churches, schools, and community event times           |
    | 4     | government stuff, finances and spending                |
    | 5     | bank info, business, and town government               |
    | 6     | national politics                                      |
    | 7     | patent medicine advertisements and sentimental writing |
    | 8     | editorials, education, and public commentary           |
    | 9     | lots of disasters, rivers, and geography               |
    | 10    | railroads and train schedules                          |
    | 11    | advertisements and produce rates                       |
    | 12    | food, household advice, and lots of dessert recipes    |
    | 13    | local people and family announcements (new babies yay) |
    | 14    | court, probate, and estate notices                     |

    Some topics were especially clear after I looked at their highest-weighted documents. Topic 10 contained New Haven and Northampton Railroad schedules, train arrival times, and travelers’ guides. Topic 9 included floods, reservoir disasters, rivers, bridges, and other stories about places or natural events. Topic 8 included articles about public schools, graduation speeches, language, local history, and general public issues, so I interpreted it as editorial and educational commentary.

    Topic 7 stayed kind of strange even after refining the model. Its two highest-weighted documents were repeated Vinegar Bitters advertisements, but several of the other documents were poems or sentimental stories about family, love, and death. This seems to be a genuinely mixed topic. It may also be partly caused by the article segmentation combining advertisements and nearby literary pieces from the same page.

    ## Discussion

    My original predictions were all kind of there. The model found local topics involving Westfield, churches, schools, businesses, courts, agriculture, and railroads. It also found a clear national politics topic containing words such as *Republican*, *president*, *Grant*, *Congress*, and *government*. The different kinds of writing I predicted also appeared, including advertisements, legal notices, local announcements, business notices, and serialized stories. However, Reconstruction and the Panic of 1873 did not appear as their own clear topics, so those predictions were probably too specific, google lied to me.

    I was surprised by how much of the newspaper consisted of fiction, poetry, religious writing, household advice, and patent medicine advertisements. Some of the largest topics were broad narrative or commentary categories instead of straightforward local news. The model also sometimes separated documents by type of writing rather than by subject. For example, legal notices, train schedules, advertisements, and fiction each had their own recognizable language and structure.

    Text mining helped because it made patterns across almost 6,000 articles visible without requiring me to read every page. At the same time, the top-word lists could be misleading when viewed by themselves. Looking at the highest-weighted documents was necessary before giving the topics meaningful labels. OCR errors, leftover formatting, repeated advertisements, and articles that contained unrelated pieces also made some topics harder to interpret. Overall, the refined model gave a useful overview of the newspaper, but the topics still needed to be checked against the original documents instead of being treated as perfect categories.

                    ## Part 3: Predictions (written before viewing the data)

    **Newspaper:** Norfolk County Journal (Massachusetts), 1849–1871

    **Predicted themes:**
    - Civil War coverage (1861–1865): troop movements, casualty lists, war finance, emancipation debate
    - Local agriculture and commerce: crop reports, livestock, local business advertisements
    - Political party coverage: given the era, likely Whig/Republican vs Democratic party news
    - Religious/moral content: church notices, temperance movement (common in this period)
    - Shipping/trade notices, given Massachusetts' maritime economy
    - Classified-style ads: land sales, auctions, runaway notices (pre-war), employment

    **Predicted article types:** short news items, political editorials, local announcements, advertisements, possibly serialized fiction or poetry (common in 19th-century papers)

    **Predicted common words:** war-related terms during 1861–1865 (war, Union, rebel, army); commerce terms (sale, county, market); formal 19th-century phrasing (whereas, hereby, esteemed)

    **Note:** No prior familiarity with this specific newspaper or region — predictions are based on general knowledge of the historical period (Civil War era Massachusetts) rather than specific research into this publication.

                Source line 89

                    ## Part 3

                Source line 139

                    ## Part 4.

                Source line 176

                    ## Part 5.

                Source line 233

                    ## Part 6: Discussion

    **What did I find vs. what I predicted?**

    Predicted Civil War coverage as a dominant theme did not appear as a distinct topic. Instead, the most interpretable topics were local civic/commercial: real estate transactions and probate court notices (Topics 1, 2), railroad schedules and corporate meetings (Topics 0, 8), municipal government (Topic 9), and social notices/obituaries (Topic 13). This suggests the paper's day-to-day content was dominated by routine local administration and commerce rather than war reporting, even though the collection period spans the Civil War.

    **Most surprising finding**

    Topic 10 keywords (state, party, government, president, congress, union) looked like national politics, but the top-scoring documents were a fire department annual report and a bank stockholders' notice. This shows topic keywords alone can be misleading. Formal civic/institutional language (annual report, meeting, president, union as in "labor union" or organizational body) overlaps vocabulary with national political discourse, even though the actual content is local administration.

    **How the techniques helped or hindered**

    Removing stopwords was essential. The first run (no stopwords) produced topics dominated entirely by function words (the, of, and, to) with no interpretable signal. After adding a stopword list and a minimum document frequency filter, topics became clearly interpretable. However, several topics (3, 4, 7, 11) remained vague even after this cleaning, suggesting either more aggressive stopword removal is needed, or that a 15-topic model is still too coarse to separate some remaining mixed themes. Checking top documents per topic (not just top words) was necessary to catch the Topic 10 misinterpretation. Word lists alone would have led to a wrong conclusion about the paper's content.

    **Limitation to note:** results are based on a random sample of 4,000 articles (out of 18,341) with a fast iteration count (300) for speed; a full-corpus run with more iterations might surface a distinct Civil War topic that this sample missed.

                Source line 60

                    The newspaper is called The Boston Atlas and it covers the years 1841-1857. Before looking at the data, some general themes I expect include: abolitionism and activism against slavery/anti-slavery movements, particularly regarding Supreme Court decisions during this time period (such as the Fugitive Slave Act); technological advancements, such as improvements to railroads and communication technology; women's suffrage and rights; and discussion around immigration.

                Source line 161

                    I experimented a lot with the number of topics (20, 25, 30) and min documents per word (5, 10, 20) and found that I got the most distinguishable results with 30 topics and 20 docs per word. I also tried increasing the iterations to 600 and found that the results were negligable in difference.

                Source line 405

                    I found that the newspaper covered a wide variety of recurring themes and article types, including politics and elections, slavery, legislation, railroads, shipping, markets, banking, and local Boston events. This mostly matched my predictions, although I definitely had forgotten to consider in my prediction the importance of daily local news such as shipping and markets. So it was interesting to see how strongly certain types of content, like train schedules, shipping notices, and commodity reports, appeared as their own topics. I was also surprised by how sensitive the results were to the modeling choices. Using fewer topics didn't necessarily make the model easier to interpret; with 20 or 25 topics, several themes were combined into large, vague topics that were very hard to distinguish, while 30 topics generally produced clearer distinctions. Changing the minimum document frequency and stopword list also helped remove possible OCR errors and formatting noise, but removing too many words sometimes made the results worse because some abbreviations actually helped identify specific types of articles. Overall, topic modeling was helpful for getting a broad overview of such a large collection, but it also sometimes made interpretation more difficult because not every topic represented a clean theme. Looking at both the highest-probability words and the articles most strongly associated with each topic was important for figuring out what the model had actually found. The UMAP visualization provided some additional visual support by showing that certain article types formed distinct groups while many others overlapped (such as the shipping and commodity articles).
