                      ### Part 6

                  Source line 361

                      In the first iteration (t = 30, i = 500), I noticed that the first top_words were all french and seemed relatively random. The only first group of words that had a common theme was at 4 (river, miles, county, new states). Number 5 discussed lots of ships, cargo, society, etc. But overall, there were not many distinct groupings of words that stuck out.

    In the next iteration (t = 20, i = 500), I noticed that the categories were increasingly more random. However two main groups stuck out: government, country, people, federal, public and states, britain, embargo. THere were also more french. At this point, I decided to add the common french words to my stop words for it to stop appearing as a category.

    The next iteration (t = 50, i = 500), I noticed a few more distinct themes: man, like, virture, human, god & government, citizens, states, united, & farmers, england, america, taxes.

    In the next iteration (t = 50, i = 300), I noticed that this had the best grouping of words by topic. The topics were mainly boston/ship/cargo, law, committe/bills/report, dollar/taxes/millions, and britain.

    The last iteration (t = 50, i = 1000), there were a lot of specific related to government, religion, society, ships, cargo, river, land, etc. This was also one of the best goruping of words.

    Overall, it looks like increasing topics and iterations makes the best grouping of words. This would make sense. I found that decreasing the topics meant that more words were clustered together and didn't follow a unique topic. I also added common words that were coming up to stop words so that we could look more at the meaning of the actual words. I think some of the themes I predicted above were shown here. There were talks of buildings, government, expansion, etc. However, I think there was less related to social justice based on the movements of the time. Also, there were a lot of mentions about ships, cargo, embargos, as well as Britain. This was not something that I had anticipated, and it surprised me how many times it showed up as a top_words grouping.

                      ## Part 6: Discussion

    The 27-topic model found several clear themes and a few non-themes. Coherent topics included: debt/fiscal language (dollars, millions, debt), Federalist party politics (federal, federalists, government, party), the Burr affair (letter, president, burr), Anglo-French trade disputes (states, france, britain, war, commerce), frontier/geography coverage (river, miles, territory, indians), and vital records (aged, miss, esq, rev). The single highest-weighted topic in the whole corpus was Embargo Act and Orders-in-Council resolutions, including the boilerplate "can an American vessel proceed..." language repeated across many issues during 1807-09. This one was immediately obvious from the top words alone.

    Some topics weren't real themes. One was just French stopwords (les, des, que, nous, pour, dans), reflecting documents written in French rather than any subject. Another was mostly modal verbs (will, can, may, must, shall) that the stoplist missed. A third (thy, shall, heart, thou, love, god) looks like a poetry column rather than a topic.

    The Part 3 predictions were off. They assumed the 1824-66 Whig, protectionist Courier, but this content (Federalists, Burr, the Embargo, Batavian Republic proclamations) is from the earlier Jeffersonian period. The general prediction that trade/politics would dominate held up, but the specifics didn't: neutral-rights and embargo politics, not tariffs; Federalists, not Whigs. Predicted commercial and civic content did show up (trade coverage, obituaries). The Geoponics/farming topic didn't clearly appear, maybe because this portion predates that column, or it's just further down the topic list.

    The most surprising find was a topic on the Batavian Republic and Franco-Dutch politics, not something predicted at all. It suggests these papers reprinted a lot of continental European political material. More broadly, the mismatch shows it's worth checking date metadata directly rather than relying on general history for a serial number, since one title can span very different eras.

    On the technique itself: the stoplist caught common function words but not long-s OCR errors ("excule" for excuse, "conful" for consul, "revrence" for reverence), which likely diluted some topics without forming their own junk topic. The French and modal-verb topics are the clearest cases of non-thematic output and are best just flagged as such. Article-type filtering seems to have worked, since the top topics are news/editorial content rather than ads. A useful next step would be rerunning without that filter to see whether ads and shipping notices form their own topics or mostly add noise.

                      # Assignment 5: Topic Modeling

    Newspaper: Times and News-Letter, Westfield, Massachusetts, 1874–1884.

    Because this is a Westfield, Massachusetts newspaper from 1874–1884, I expect frequent local notices about town meetings, churches, schools, businesses, and transportation. I also expect advertisements, obituaries, and reports on state and national politics. Words such as Westfield, town, meeting, church, railroad, and sale may occur often. Some topics may capture recurring newspaper sections, such as advertisements, rather than a single subject.

                  Source line 159

                      ## Part 4: Analyze

                  Source line 201

                      ### Inspect uncertain topics

                  Source line 233

                      ## Part 5: Iterate

                  Source line 332

                      ## Part 6: Discuss

                  Source line 340

                      I modeled 10,763 sections from the Times and News-Letter pages labeled Westfield, Massachusetts, from 1874–1884. I approximated article boundaries using # headings in the OCR text and excluded sections shorter than 80 words. My initial 10-topic model found recognizable patterns, including recipes, market prices, and probate notices, but several topics were dominated by common words, HTML table tags, or repeated advertisements.

    Before reading the collection, I predicted coverage of local institutions, transportation, commerce, and public affairs, alongside advertisements and notices. The models support much of that prediction. The 16-topic model separated local churches and schools, retail market listings, agriculture, legal and property notices, and election coverage more clearly than the 10-topic model. I had expected transportation to form a clear theme, but a topic containing train and place names also assigned high weight to insurance advertisements and other notices. Its top words alone would have led me to overstate that theme.

    The most surprising result was how strongly newspaper format and repetition shaped the topics. Four of the five strongest documents in one 10-topic cluster were copies of the same insurance advertisement from different dates. Another topic was driven partly by td and tr, which came from HTML table markup rather than newspaper language. Removing markup and expanding the stoplist improved readability. Increasing the topic count from 10 to 16 made agriculture and election coverage easier to distinguish, but some topics still mixed unrelated sections.

    Topic modeling helped me survey thousands of sections and find recurring forms of content. Inspecting high-weighted documents was essential: a plausible word list sometimes hid an ad, a mixed section, or an OCR error. The heading-based splits are approximate, and repeated notices may have disproportionate influence. These topics describe patterns in the processed text, so I would not treat their weights as exact measures of what the newspaper or its readers cared about.

                      ## Part 6: Discussion

    I think my prediction still matches a large part of the topics. For example, topic 14 was strongly associated with government and politics, with words such as “bank,” “state,” “public,” “government,” “president,” “Congress,” and “United.” Some other topics I forgot to mention show words about agriculture and rural life, like “soil,” “crop,” “corn,” “land,” “wheat,” “cattle,” “sheep,” “hay,” and “milk.” Literary and writing, money, science, labor, and industry topics are all appeared in the list.

    Some surprising founding was that some topics was not a clearly identifiable subject. Its common words includes “may,” “time,” “every,” “great,” “subject,” “matter,” and “different.” In addition, , some token were not even words, "td", "tr", "pr", "bbl". I guess that may imply a topic model does not always produce a clean human-readable theme. It just catches general writing style and repeated patterns rather than a specific subject. Or, it may be simply affected by OCR errors. Agriculture was also more prominent than I initially expected, perhaps due to the fact I live in city.

    The topic model helped me understand the collection by assigning each document a mixture of topic probabilities. The top words technique is most helpful by giving an initial description of each topic, while the SQL queries made it possible to interpret those topics using actual article titles and text. Looking at topics by year also provided a way to examine how the newspaper’s content changed over time.

                      #Predictions:
    Based on the location and period of The Springfield Union (1884–1898), this corpus is expected to reflect Springfield's role as a major late-Gilded Age manufacturing center and New England railroad junction  (for the record, I didn't know some of these off the topic of my head, but simply searched up "Late 1800s Springfield Massachusetts" and did some research).                       ## Part 4: Query Analysis & Findings

    ### 1. Top Words (Topic 0)
    **Top 10 Words:** `man` (1.35%), `little` (0.99%), `old` (0.75%), `see` (0.72%), `know` (0.69%), `go` (0.62%), `day` (0.61%), `never` (0.60%), `time` (0.56%), `good` (0.54%)

    I used topic 0 (for simplicity) for this analysis. Topic is mostly everyday, storytelling language rather than government or business talk. Because the words are simple actions and descriptions like "little," "old," "go," and "day," this topic probably picks up short stories, local gossip, and casual reading pieces in the paper.

    ---

    ### 2. Associated Documents (Topic 0)
    **Top Headlines:** `# LETTERS Uncalled for in the Post Office` (73.69%), `# CONNECTICUT RIVER RAIL-ROAD` (70.70%), `# PERSONAL` (69.75%), `# VERMONT` (68.55%), `# MEDICINAL` (66.98%)

    Articles most strongly associated with Topic 0 predominantly appear on inner and back pages (such as pages 6, 7, and 12), containing short notices like unclaimed mail, personal columns, and medicinal briefs. Rather than major front-page news, these documents represent standard everyday regional inqueries.

    ---

    ### 3. Topic Prevalence by Year (1884)
    * **Top Topics in 1884:** Topic 24 (18 articles), Topic 19 (11 articles), Topic 0 (10 articles), Topic 17 (8 articles), Topic 7 (7 articles), Topic 15 (7 articles), Topic 23 (6 articles), Topic 12 (5 articles)

    In the 1884 sample, general public interest stories (Topic 24) and railroad/stock quotes (Topic 19) lead in article count, followed closely by miscellaneous filler (Topic 0) and directory listings of names (Topic 17) The remaining distribution shows an expected split across local court reports (Topic 23) and political party election coverage (Topic 12).

    ---

    ### 4. Search Query: 'railroad'
    * **Top Associated Topics:** Topic 27 (rank 4, 1.01%), Topic 18 (rank 20, 0.94%), Topic 29 (rank 22, 0.69%), Topic 8 (rank 31, 0.68%), Topic 19 (rank 58, 0.37%)

    The keyword query shows that the model successfully divided references to railroads across distinct operational contexts. It isolated physical train fires and accidents (Topic 27), passenger rail lines like the Hartford and New Haven lines (Topic 18), corporate railway financing (Topic 29), and river transit schedules (Topic 8) instead of lumping them into a single generic theme.

                  Source line 404

                      # Part 6: Discussion

    ### Findings vs. Initial Predictions
    My initial guess for *The Springfield Union* (1884–1898) was that we would see a lot of coverage on trains, local factories, city politics, and the 1898 war. The model mostly backed this up. The queries found clear topics for train routes and schedules (Topics 8, 18, 27), state and national elections (Topic 12), and city police court cases (Topic 23). The word "railroad" showed up exactly where expected across travel, finance, and accident reports.

    ### What Was Most Surprising?
    The biggest surprise was how much of the newspaper was just everyday filler, short stories, and local notices rather than big news events. Topic 0 was dominated by basic conversational words like "man," "little," "old," and "good," capturing things like unclaimed mail and personal notes. It was also surprising how the model handled scanner glitches: instead of ignoring bad scans, it grouped broken words and typos together into their own junk topics.

    ### How Text Mining Helped and Hindered
    * **How it helped:** It let me scan through thousands of pages in a few minutes without having to read them manually. It also handled mixed topics well, showing how one page could contain crime news, train tables, and ads all at the same time.
    * **How it hindered:** The model only counts word pairs and doesn't understand context. Because of that, repetitive store ads and messy OCR typos easily cluttered the output, meaning I had to manually filter out extra filler words so the real topics could stand out.

    *Please Note: AI was used to format these responses (responses are made by me though ☺️)

                      ## Part 3 (a): Make predictions

    Before examining the results of the topic model, I predict that the
    *Westfield Daily Times* will contain a mixture of local news, national
    news, and non-news content such as serialized fiction and advertisements.

    Because this is an early 1900s (1906-1907) newspaper from a small
    Massachusetts mill town near Springfield, I expect themes including:

    - Local community news: church, grange, and society events
    - Local government and civic announcements (probate notices, voter
      registration, town meetings)
    - Railroads and industry, given the region's manufacturing economy
    - National news and politics, likely reprinted wire service copy from
      larger cities like Boston, New York, and Washington
    - Crime, court cases, and accidents
    - Sports, especially baseball and local recreational leagues
    - Serialized fiction or literary content, common in newspapers of this era
    - Advertisements and classified notices

    I predict that words related to church, street, town, committee, state,
    government, company, and railroad will be common, alongside proper nouns
    for nearby towns (Springfield, Holyoke, Boston) given the paper's
    regional focus.

    I expect the resulting topics to be mixtures rather than perfectly
    separated modern categories. In particular, I expect some topics to
    reflect writing style or genre (e.g. formal civic language vs. narrative
    prose) rather than a single clean subject, and some local recurring
    features (like a weekly schedule or column) may appear as their own
    topic rather than blending into a broader theme.

                      ## Part 6: Discuss

                  Source line 1057

                      **What I found**

    The topics split roughly into four groups:

    - Hyper-local, formulaic content (Topics 0, 5, 7, 8, about 32% of the corpus): railroad timetables, church and grange notices, probate and estate notices, voter registration, and local bowling and whist leagues. These are easy to interpret because they come from recurring, structured newspaper features rather than open-ended writing.
    - Serialized fiction (Topic 3, 11.5%): syndicated novels that ran in installments across issues.
    - Wire-service national news (Topic 4, 11%): coverage out of New York, Washington, and Boston, mixed in with baseball box scores.
    - Crime and courts (Topic 6, 9%): larger than expected, driven mostly by a couple of major stories (the "Al Adams" murder case and references to the Thaw trial).
    - Infrastructure and industry (Topic 9, 11%): railroads, fires, buildings, and water works, consistent with a mill-town economy.

    The two largest topics did not correspond to a clean subject:

    - Topic 1 (17%) reads as formal civic and government language rather than a specific theme.
    - Topic 2 (15%) reads as general narrative prose that shows up in both editorials and fiction.

    **Comparing to my predictions**

    Most of my Part 3 predictions held up:

    - I expected local news, civic/government content, railroads and industry, crime, sports, and national wire coverage. All of these appeared as distinct topics.
    - I expected serialized fiction to show up. It did, and as a larger, more clearly separated topic than I anticipated, making up over 11% of the corpus on its own.
    - I expected some topics to reflect writing style rather than a single clear subject. Topics 1 and 2 confirmed this, since they are really about register (formal vs. narrative) more than content.
    - I expected recurring local features to form their own topics instead of blending into broader themes. This happened with the railroad timetable and probate notice topics.

    **What was most surprising**

    - The size and separation of the fiction topic stood out most. It's a reminder that newspapers of this era were a major distribution channel for serialized novels, not just a source of news.
    - Several topics amounted to "one recurring column" rather than a genuine theme. LDA cannot really tell the difference between a coherent subject and a formulaic feature that repeats the same vocabulary every issue, so a weekly train schedule can look statistically just as strong as a real topic.
    - OCR and formatting noise leaked into at least one topic. Topic 8 contains terms like "td," "tr," and "th," which are leftover HTML table tags from scanned box scores.

    **How the techniques helped or hindered analysis**

    - Top words alone were often not enough to interpret a topic. Topic 2's top words, for example, could plausibly describe an obituary, an editorial, or a piece of fiction.
    - Cross-checking against the top-weighted documents for each topic was necessary to figure out what a topic actually represented. The word lists gave vocabulary, but the documents gave genre.
    - Comparing models with 5, 10, and 20 topics showed a tradeoff: more topics split the vague "register" topics into more specific ones, but also fragmented the results overall.
    - Adding stopwords such as "said," "new," "year," "day," and "time" helped clean up the largest, most generic topics somewhat, though it did not fully separate style from subject matter.
    - Removing OCR artifact terms like "td," "tr," and "th" would likely improve Topic 8 specifically and is a good candidate for further stopword tuning.

                      ## Part 5: Iterate
    *(Notes and experiments: try varying topic count $k$, modifying the stoplist with common OCR artifacts or frequent domain words, and adjusting minimum article lengths).*

    Observations from our initial run (k=30 topics):
        - Most topics were surprisingly coherent and interpretable, representing clear categories like baseball (topic 12), boxing (topic 15), women's fashion (topic 6), real estate (topic 13), cooking recipes (topic 7), and city politics (topic 8).
        - Topic 28 stood out as an obvious OCR / parsing artifact: its top words are "td tr th table tbody thead score totals team currier", which are raw HTML table tags from scanned box scores. Adding "td", "tr", "th", "table", "tbody", and "thead" to the stopword list eliminates this junk topic.
        - Several general words like "boston", "to-day", "today", "yesterday", "man", and "new" appear across multiple distinct topics. Adding these high-frequency domain words to the stopword list helps the model find more distinctive vocabulary for each theme.
        - Choosing k=30 gave a good level of granularity, although some broader subjects split into multiple sub-topics (for example, stock market prices appeared in both topic 5 and topic 23, and sports split across baseball in topic 12, college sports in topic 22, and boxing in topic 15).

                  Source line 475

                      ## Part 6: Discuss
    What did you find in the collection?

    I analyzed The Boston American from 1908–1911 (over 17,000 pages and 117,000 articles).
    The 30 topics revealed a vivid snapshot of early 20th-century city life:
    - Politics: Local Boston mayoral and ward politics (Topic 8) and national news like President Taft and legislation (Topic 4).
    - Crime & Legal: Police blotters and accidents (Topic 2) and court trials (Topic 10).
    - Sports: Major League baseball (Topic 12), prize boxing (Topic 15), and college sports (Topic 22).
    - Everyday Culture: Women's fashion and gowns (Topic 6), cooking recipes (Topic 7), real estate deeds (Topic 13), classified job ads (Topic 0), and serialized romantic fiction stories (Topics 17 & 26).

    Compare your actual findings to your guesses.

    They matched our predictions almost exactly. Before looking at the data, I predicted that a 1910 Boston newspaper would feature local city government, crime/police reports, baseball/boxing, classified ads, and society news. The model proved that these exact themes made up the bulk of the newspaper's daily content (e.g., Topic 12 was >95% pure baseball recaps, Topic 15 was pure boxing, Topic 8 was city politics).

    What was most surprising?
    - The Jack Johnson Boxing Match (Topic 15): The model didn't just find general sports; it isolated a specific historic event—the massive media frenzy surrounding the famous July 4, 1910 heavyweight championship fight between Jack Johnson and James Jeffries.
    - The North Pole Controversy (Topic 27): Discovered the public obsession with Dr. Frederick Cook's 1909 claim of discovering the North Pole.
    - The HTML Table Tags Topic (Topic 28): A fun technical surprise where OCR accidentally captured raw HTML tags (td, tr, th, table, tbody) from sports box score tables, and the model clustered them into their own distinct "topic."
    - Serialized Fiction (Topics 17 & 26): Showing how much space daily newspapers devoted to short stories and romantic drama before radio and TV existed.

    Describe how the text mining techniques you applied helped (or hindered) you in finding and understanding patterns.

    Text mining techniques applied gave an instant, high-level summary of an enormous historical archive (117,000 articles) in minutes and the SQL queries made it easy to drill straight from high-level word patterns down to the exact headlines and dates (like catching the 1909 Red Sox season finale).
    However, the model is purely statistical and doesn't know what HTML tags are, so junk like <td> and <tr> formed a topic. Common words like boston, today, man, and new appeared in multiple topics, diluting their uniqueness unless filtered out by custom stopwords. Historical newspapers are full pages, not neat articles, so heuristics were needed to merge small headline fragments together.

    - **Commerce and shipping:** ship arrivals, cargo, prices, cotton, railroads, banks, insurance

                  Source line 162

                      ## Part 4: Analyze

                  Source line 235

                      ## Part 5: Iterate

    - **k = 10:** topics were too broad; politics, war, and foreign news merged together.
    - **k = 20:** best balance; most topics had a clear theme.
    - **k = 40:** some good narrow topics (e.g. one for ship arrivals, one for court cases), but also several near-duplicate and junk OCR topics.
    - **Stoplist:** the first run had topics full of OCR fragments (*tbe, aud, iu, nnd*) and abbreviations (*inst, ult*). Adding them to the stoplist freed those topics up for real content.

    ## Part 6: Discuss

    **What I found:** The model separated the paper into political news (Whig/Democrat, Congress, elections), shipping and commerce, advertisements and sales, local Boston news, foreign news, and war/Mexico coverage. Several topics are not themes at all but **document types**, like price lists or ads, which share vocabulary rather than subject.

    **Predictions vs. reality:** Politics, commerce, and ads matched my guesses well.

    **Most surprising:** How much of the paper is ads and lists, and that OCR errors formed their own topics.

    **How the methods helped or hindered:** Topic modeling gave a fast overview that would take days to get by reading. Mixed membership fit better than k-means from A4, because one article often mixes politics and war. But results depend a lot on k and the stoplist, OCR noise leaks into topics, and top words alone can mislead; reading the top documents with the SQL queries was necessary to name topics correctly. The PCA plot from A3 showed which topics overlap.

                      # Assignment 5: Topic Modeling

    A "dataset unboxing" of two Boston newspapers from the Boston Public Library OCR collection, sixty years apart.

    ## Part 1: Installation

    `pyrmallet` is installed with `pip install pyrmallet` and imported above along with the other packages this notebook uses.

                  Source line 55

                      ## Part 2: Select newspapers

    I picked two runs from `index.json` that should differ as much as possible while both being Boston dailies, so the topic model has a built-in contrast to recover:

    | paper | file | years | pages in index |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | **The Atlas** (Boston) | `The_Atlas__sn84022190.jsonl.gz` | 1841 to 1844 | 4,467 |
    | **Hearst's Boston American** | `HEARST_S_BOSTON_AMERICAN__sn91057091.jsonl.gz` | 1904 to 1905 | 5,795 |

    The Atlas was the main Whig party paper in Boston in the era of Harrison, Tyler and Henry Clay. Hearst's Boston American was William Randolph Hearst's new popular daily, the Boston outpost of "yellow journalism".

                  Source line 118

                      ## Part 3a: Predictions (written before looking at any model output)

    **The Atlas, Boston, 1841 to 1844.** This is a Whig party paper in a port city during a very political stretch: the 1840 "log cabin" campaign had just been won, Harrison died a month into office, Tyler broke with the Whigs, and Clay ran in 1844. I expect:

    * **Party politics** to be the biggest theme: *whig, whigs, loco, locofoco, democrat, clay, tyler, van buren, convention, election, vote, ticket, party*.
    * **Congress and legislature** proceedings: *senate, house, bill, committee, motion, tariff, bank, resolution*.
    * **Shipping and maritime commerce**, since Boston is a port: *ship, brig, schooner, arrived, sailed, cargo, captain, wharf, marine*.
    * **Markets and prices**: *flour, cotton, sugar, molasses, bbls, sales, prices, market*.
    * **Advertisements and legal notices**: *sale, store, street, notice, estate, auction, subscriber, administrator*.
    * **Early railroads**: *railroad, trains, depot, passengers, Worcester, Lowell, Providence*.
    * Smaller themes on **Texas annexation, slavery and abolition, temperance, religion**, and foreign news from **England** by steamer.

    **Hearst's Boston American, 1904 to 1905.** A mass-market "yellow" paper with pictures and sections. I expect:

    * **Crime and courts**: *police, murder, arrested, court, judge, trial, woman, body*.
    * **Sports**: *game, team, baseball, league, Harvard, football, won, inning*.
    * **Society, theatre and women's pages**: *miss, mrs, wedding, theatre, gown, lace, fashion*.
    * **Local Boston politics and labor**: *mayor, city, council, union, strike, labor, Hearst himself* (he ran for the 1904 Democratic nomination).
    * **International news**: the **Russo-Japanese War** (*japanese, russian, port arthur, fleet*).
    * **Department store advertising** and **finance** tables (*stocks, bonds, railroad shares*).
    * Serial fiction and human-interest stories with ordinary narrative words (*man, little, know, life*).

    **Types of article I expect:** editorials, letters from Washington, congressional reports, ship arrivals ("marine journal"), price currents, railroad timetables, legal and bankruptcy notices, and many ads in the Atlas. News stories with headlines, sports reports, society columns, features, fiction, and display ads in the American. The two papers should mostly separate into different topics, with a few shared "newspaper" topics such as fires, accidents, and courts.

                  Source line 148

                      ### Segmentation

    I use the example notebook's segmentation unchanged: a new article starts at any line beginning with `#` (a heading in the OCR markdown), and fragments are merged until each article is at least 800 characters.

                  Source line 205

                      Segmentation produced **{expression}** articles
    ({expression}),
    median length {expression} characters.

    To keep training time reasonable and to stop the Atlas (whose four-page broadsheets hold far more text per page) from dominating,
    I train on a random sample of **{expression} articles per paper** ({expression} total).

                  Source line 227

                      ## Part 3b: Train a topic model

    The stoplist starts from the example notebook's list. The training helper does three things beyond calling `LatentDirichletAllocation`:

    1. It builds the `pyrmallet` corpus once up front to find articles that end up with **zero tokens** after stopwords and the minimum-document-frequency cutoff (for example, an article that was only numbers). `pyrmallet` silently drops those, which would misalign rows of `doc_topic_distributions_` with the article table, so I remove them first.
    2. It uses a **fixed random seed**, so the results the discussion describes are reproducible.
    3. It **caches** each fitted model's matrices in `models/`, so reopening the notebook does not retrain four models. Deleting that folder retrains everything from scratch.

                  Source line 370

                      The baseline is already readable: shipping news, market prices, Whig party politics, railroad timetables, sports and police news all appear as recognizable topics. Several top-word lists are cluttered, though, and one topic is pure formatting:

    * Tables in the OCR are written as HTML, so one topic is literally *td tr th table tbody thead* (stock and sports tables from the American).
    * The ordinal suffixes *th, nd, rd* are everywhere, because the tokenizer drops digits and "12th" becomes "th".
    * *may* is both a month and a verb, and abbreviations like *wm, chas, jr, esq, inst* (*inst* means "instant", or "of this month") show up in many topics.
    * Generic newspaper filler (*new, last, great, first, time, day, every, must, shall, little, good, years*) sits near the top of many unrelated topics.

    Model 2 adds all of these to the stoplist and becomes the main model for analysis.

                  Source line 408

                      ## Part 5: Iterate

    Two kinds of iteration beyond the stoplist change above:

    * **Number of topics.** Models 3 and 4 use the same stoplist as the main model with 15 and 60 topics.
    * **An earlier technique, TF-IDF (Assignment 2).** As a cross-check on the topics, I compare the words that best distinguish the two papers under TF-IDF against the Atlas-heavy and Hearst-heavy topics.

                  Source line 673

                      **Lining up the three models.** For each of the 30 main topics, I find its closest topic in the 15-topic and 60-topic models by cosine similarity of their word distributions (all three share the same vocabulary, since they use the same stoplist and documents). When two 30-topic topics map to the same 15-topic topic, the smaller model merged them. When a 30-topic topic has only a weak best match at 60, the larger model split it.

                  Source line 727

                      ### Free-form query

    Edit the SQL below to explore further, for example searching `word_topics` for a word or `documents` for a headline.

                  Source line 752

                      ## Part 6: Discuss

    **What is in the collection.** The two papers separate clearly. 17 of the 30 main topics have an `atlas_share` above 0.8 or below 0.3, so they mostly belong to one paper. Most of the rest are either general vocabulary that both papers share or small OCR-artifact topics (see below). The Atlas side is a commercial and political paper. Its biggest topics are timetables for the new railroads (Lowell, Worcester, Providence, Springfield and Albany lines), ship arrivals and clearances ("sch", "brig", "arr"), price currents for flour, cotton and molasses, legal notices (probate, bankruptcy, copartnership), and Whig party politics ("whig", "loco", "convention", "majority"). By article count, railroad timetables and legal or business notices are the top Atlas topics in every year from 1841 to 1844. The page query shows the layout: political and "general news" topics sit on pages 1 and 2, and railroad ads average close to page 4, the back page. Hearst's American is a city paper about people. Its biggest topics are sports (baseball, Harvard football, racing and boxing), police and court stories, human-interest narrative ("man, life, young, woman, know"), theatre and society news, and Boston labor and ward politics. Sports, police and court news, and human-interest narrative are the top three Hearst topics by article count in both 1904 and 1905.

    **Predictions vs. findings.** Most of the broad predictions held. Party politics, Congress, shipping, markets, legal notices and railroads were all clean Atlas topics, and sports, crime, society and theatre, and labor were clear Hearst topics. The word-level check confirms *whig, loco, brig, flour, railroad, auction* land in Atlas topics and *police, harvard, strike, theatre* in Hearst topics. Three predictions failed. First, the reform issues I expected in the Atlas (*texas, slavery, abolition, temperance*) are not in the top 100 words of any topic at k = 30. They are either spread thin across the general political topic or simply not a big share of the paper. Second, the Russo-Japanese War is only a small topic and is polluted by unrelated words at k = 30. It only becomes clean at k = 60 ("war japanese russian general army"). Third, Hearst's own 1904 presidential campaign and Boston mayoral politics only get their own topic at k = 60 ("democratic hearst governor mayor republican"). I also underestimated how much of the Atlas is advertising and notices rather than editorial writing.

    **What was most surprising.** The biggest surprise was how much a few OCR errors shaped the model. Several topics mix good words with meaningless names, like *union george twin catharine labor* or *gray foreign letter jacob japanese russian ... satisfying nutritious*. The documents query shows why. The top documents for these topics are articles where the OCR engine got stuck in a loop and repeated one phrase over a thousand times ("foreign letter" 1,295 times, "Jacob McMullen" 1,368 times, "Twin George ... Catharine" about 1,400 times). LDA treats each of those tokens as real evidence, so a single broken article can take over most of a topic. Even after the stoplist removed the `td`/`tr` table topic, table-heavy stock listings still formed their own small topic. The second surprise was the difference in mixing. 42% of Atlas articles are at least 80% one topic, but only 8% of Hearst articles are. Atlas content is formulaic (a timetable is only a timetable), while Hearst stories blend crime, politics, and human interest in the same article.

    **How the techniques helped or hindered.** The topic model was a fast way to "unbox" 10,000 pages I could never read, and the SQL queries made it easy to go from a word list back to real articles, which is where the interpretation actually happens. Iterating was essential. Adding stopwords turned formatting and filler topics into readable ones. Changing k showed which themes are stable: the shipping, market and railroad topics find a match with cosine similarity of 0.8 or higher at both k = 15 and k = 60, and sports matches at 0.95 and 0.75. Other themes only exist at some scales. At k = 15 all of Atlas politics merges into one "state whig house party bill" topic, and at k = 60 the Hearst paper splits into war, politics, romance and fashion topics. TF-IDF from Assignment 2 agreed with the topics about what separates the papers (*leave, trains, rail, notice* vs. *to-day, game, police, woman*). It also showed the limitation of a single contrast list: it cannot tell you that these words form distinct themes. The main hindrances were the OCR loops and the fact that topics do not always mean "theme". Some topics are genres or page formats (timetables, tables, name lists), and some are artifacts of a single bad document. Without reading the highest-weighted documents, I would have misread those topics as real themes.

                  Source line 511

                      ### What did you find in the collection?
    The topics ranged between politics, transportation/places, business/finance, government, war, books/literature, people/names, railroad schedules, national news, crime, family/personal stories, health/medicine, poetry/religion and local notices.

    ### Compare your actual findings to your guesses. What was most surprising?
    I was suprised that with 25 topics one of the topics was purely names. I didn't think that this would be a topic but after seeing it it makes sense that a news article would have so many names and that these would all be grouped together. I was correct that there was a topic for politics and war but there wasn't a topic for infrastructure or railroad construction. The timeframe for these articles was right after the Industrial Revolution but that may have meant that most construction was done and settled which was why there were topics about transportation itself but more about the schedules. There were also always some topic that seemed miscellenious, whether the topic number was small or large. Overall, I think the general gist of the articles is it covers a wide variety of topics because news articles often have many different sections, for example sections for family and global news, etc. The topics that I found to be prevalent made sense for the context of the newspaper and but in my guesses I didn't account for all of them.

    ### Describe how the text mining techniques you applied helped (or hindered) you in finding and understanding patterns.
    Using stop words helped get rid of common words that wouldn't add much meaning to the groupings/results. Using LDA helped segment the words into distinct groups and even though there isn't a specific label for each topic, it shows the top words so we can interpret what the general theme of the group is. These topics can helps us get a better understanding of what all these articles are primarily about and the themes they are trying to convey. Also seeing the probability distributions for both documents and words for each topic helped understand both what words are associated with what topics as well as what documents are associated with topics which gives both patterns for vocabulary and individual articles.

                  Source line 151

                      ## 2. Model settings

    Boston American runs ~17k pages / ~100k+ articles once segmented — much
    bigger than the single-file examples in the demo notebook — so training
    with the default settings below can take a while (tens of minutes on a
    laptop). Start with a smaller `Number of topics` / `Gibbs iterations` to
    iterate quickly, then do a longer final run.

    The stopword list below starts from the demo's English stopwords, plus
    additions I found necessary after inspecting this corpus's OCR output:
    single letters (`a`, `b`, `c`, ...) that show up constantly as OCR noise
    from column rules and abbreviations, a couple of very frequent OCR
    mis-reads (`td`, `tr`), and day/month names, which otherwise dominate
    topics purely because every dateline contains one.

                      ## Part 6 — Discussion


    ### Predictions vs. findings

    I found that things relating to the city, such as the policy and crime, real estate, Boston-related topics, and names such as Cambridge, Somerville, etc. all came through clearly.
    Some things which I did not expect were that things such as boxing and Jack Johnson would be so prominent.


    The techniques used worked well, but it is noisy and sometimes creates heading breaks mid-article, and some boundaries are missed. Stopword tuning mattered more than I expected for OCR'd historical text.

    Overall, the topic modeling was a good dataset unboxing tool.

                      Part 3: Make predictions

                  Source line 31

                      According to index.json, the Bunker Hill Times were a Boston area newspaper that was published from 1832-1878. I think that some of the themes that will be present are local news(i.e. things like local elections or people promoting local events), also things like weather reports. I am interested to see the language used in the paper as the wording used then was very different than modern times. I also think outside of local news, they would publish some more national type of news, such as stock market reports or changes made by the president or other governing bodies(of the US or other countries that would affect the US). I would also expect many newspaper advertisements, for local and global brands.

                  Source line 288

                      PART 4- ANALYZE

                  Source line 320

                      Part 6: Discuss

    After analysis of the Bunker Hill Times, I saw many topics such as local news, government, community events, and entertainment. This was quite similar to my original predictions.

    I was suprised by the variety of types of articles mixed into the newspaper, ranging from entertainment, to historical pieces, to government related sections. Some topics served easier to identify than others, with words like state, government, and country pertaining to government sections while words like theatre and stage clearly related more to entertainment.

    I started by using 30 topics, but got curious and changed it to 20 and added words to the stoplist. In doing so, though some topics were still harder to distinguish with more vague words, in general the topics generated by the topic model were more easily interpretable. I can't necessairly attrbute this to the addition to stoplist or reduction of topics though since i changed both simultaneously.

    I really liked topic modeling for this as if i was to manually look at these thousands of articles and try to identify topics, it would be near impossible. Overall topic modeling was useful for finding broad patterns in articles across the newspaper. Something like topic 0's words like us, life, love, heart can help it be identified as poetry but Topic 2 having words like politics seemed a bit misleading as it was more about local news with politics just being a smaller part so some level of human interpretation was needed.

                      ## Part 2: Select a newspaper

                  Source line 104

                      ## Part 3: Predictions made before reading the collection

    **Selected newspaper:** *Times and News-Letter*, published in Westfield,
    Massachusetts from 1884 to 1924.

    **Predictions:** Before examining the newspaper text, I predict that the
    collection will contain local government and election news, business and
    property advertisements, legal notices, church and community events,
    agriculture, manufacturing, railroad transportation, crime reports,
    marriages, deaths, and school news. Because the date range includes the
    early twentieth century, I also expect later issues to discuss World War I,
    women's suffrage, Prohibition, and the 1918 influenza pandemic. Likely common
    words include *Westfield*, *Springfield*, *Massachusetts*, *town*, *street*,
    *church*, *company*, and *county*. I expect OCR errors caused by old
    typefaces, narrow columns, damaged pages, and end-of-line hyphenation.

                  Source line 317

                      ## Part 3: Train a topic model

    `pyrmallet` performs its own tokenization and vocabulary construction, so
    the model receives a Python list containing one raw-text string per article.
    The stop list below begins with scikit-learn's English stop words and adds
    a small number of generic newspaper/OCR words. Revise the custom words after
    inspecting the first model—do not remove meaningful historical vocabulary
    merely because it is unfamiliar or uncomfortable.

                  Source line 644

                      ## Part 5: Iterate over topic counts

    The following cell trains models with several topic counts. It may take a
    while on a large corpus. Topic diversity is the fraction of unique words
    among the top words of all topics. Mean dominant weight measures how strongly
    documents tend to favor one topic. These are diagnostics rather than a single
    objective definition of the “best” model; interpret the actual words and
    documents as well.

                  Source line 724

                      ### Stop-list iteration

    After inspecting the top words above, add corpus-wide boilerplate and obvious
    OCR fragments to `SECOND_PASS_WORDS`. Do not simply remove words because they
    appear frequently; repeated words can be historically meaningful.

                  Source line 798

                      ## Part 6: Discussion

    ### Findings

    The refined model revealed several recurring themes in the *Times and News-Letter*. One clear theme involved **probate and legal notices**, characterized by words such as *court, county, Westfield, estate, Hampden, deceased, notice,* and *probate*. Another topic represented **land sales and property announcements**, with words including *land, tract, described, situated, street,* and *sale*. These topics reflect formal notices about estates, deceased residents, property boundaries, and public sales.

    The model also identified **local community and religious life**. One topic included *church, rev, Sunday, service, evening, town,* and *home*, while another included *meeting, Westfield, Springfield, school,* and *street*. These topics appear to represent church announcements, community meetings, and reports about local residents. A related **education** topic contained *school, schools, teachers, education, inquiry,* and *teacher*.

    Other recognizable themes included **politics**, represented by *state, house, vote, Republican, party, committee,* and *president*; **agriculture and food**, represented by *water, milk, food, farm,* and *stock*; and **health and medicine**, represented by *doctor, disease, physicians, health,* and *days*. The model also found a distinct **baseball** topic containing *hit, ball, base, game,* and *captain*, as well as a **transportation and travel-schedule** topic containing *Westfield, leave, arrive, Northampton, Holyoke,* and *New York*. Together, these topics indicate that the collection contains a mixture of news stories, legal notices, local announcements, advertisements, schedules, sports reports, and practical advice.

    ### Comparison with predictions

    Before examining the collection, I predicted that the newspaper would contain local government and election news, business and property advertisements, legal notices, church and community events, agriculture, railroad transportation, crime reports, marriages, deaths, and school news. Many of these predictions were supported. The legal and probate topic contained words such as *court, estate, deceased,* and *notice*. The property topic included *land, tract,* and *sale*. The political topic contained *vote, Republican, party,* and *committee*. Church, school, agricultural, and transportation topics also appeared clearly.

    The expected geographic vocabulary was especially prominent. *Westfield, Springfield, Hampden, Northampton,* and *Holyoke* appeared across multiple topics, confirming the local focus of the newspaper.

    However, World War I, women’s suffrage, Prohibition, and the 1918 influenza pandemic did not emerge as clear dominant topics. These subjects may occur in the collection without appearing frequently enough to define major topics, or they may be mixed into broader political and health topics. I did not anticipate that baseball would form such a distinct topic. I was also surprised by the prominence of health-remedy language such as *disease, physicians, fever,* and *dizziness*, which may reflect medical advertisements as well as health reporting.

    ### Iteration

    I compared models containing 10, 20, and 30 topics. The 10-topic model had the highest mean dominant-topic weight, approximately **0.648**, and the highest top-word diversity, **0.84**. However, it was too broad for interpretation. For example, local news, schools, churches, family announcements, and towns frequently appeared together rather than forming distinct themes.

    The 20-topic model had a lower mean dominant-topic weight of approximately **0.506** and a diversity score of **0.79**, but it separated the collection into more recognizable categories. It distinguished legal notices, politics, education, religion, transportation, health, agriculture, business, and conversational or narrative writing. For that reason, I found 20 topics to be the best balance between detail and interpretability.

    The 30-topic model had the lowest mean dominant-topic weight, approximately **0.448**, and a slightly lower diversity score of approximately **0.787**. It separated several useful themes, such as baseball and land sales, but it also produced overlapping or fragmented topics. Multiple topics contained similar words about churches, towns, people, and narrative writing. Some topics were dominated by OCR fragments rather than coherent subjects.

    After examining the first model, I added words such as *thing, like, th, st, use, did, td, tr,* and *dis* to the second-pass stop list. Many of these were generic expressions, abbreviations, table markers, or OCR artifacts. Removing them made topics such as baseball, probate notices, politics, education, agriculture, and transportation easier to recognize. The improvement was incomplete because fragments such as *ing, ed, dat, fur,* and *uv* still appeared in some refined topics, showing that additional stop-word refinement could improve the results further.

    ### Strengths and limitations

    Topic modeling helped reveal repeated themes across thousands of newspaper articles much faster than manual reading. It separated several recurring types of content, including probate notices, property sales, political reporting, school news, church announcements, health material, baseball reports, agricultural information, and transportation schedules. Comparing multiple topic counts also showed how the level of detail changes as more topics are requested.

    However, LDA topics are probability distributions over words rather than objective categories. Topic labels therefore required interpretation based on both the top words and representative documents. Some articles may also contain mixtures of several topics. OCR errors produced fragments such as *ing, ed, dat,* and *uv*, while imperfect article boundaries may have combined unrelated material from the same newspaper page. Advertisements, schedules, tables, and repeated newspaper language could also receive their own topics even when they were not conventional news articles.

    Therefore, the model was most useful as an exploratory tool for locating patterns and identifying sections of the collection for closer reading. Its results should not be treated as a complete description of the newspaper without checking representative articles and considering the historical context.

                      Predictions for 19th-Century Newspaper Recipes:

    I expect a distinct "recipe" or "domestic economy" theme to emerge, though it will likely be a minor topic compared to politics, shipping news, or general advertisements.
    Terminology: I expect the word "receipt" to be highly weighted, as this was the common 19th-century term for a recipe.
    Measurements & Ingredients: I predict informal volumetric measurements ("teacup", "spoonful", "pinch") and historically common staples ("lard", "molasses", "mutton", "suet", "flour", "boil").
    Themes & Context: These recipes will likely cluster alongside household management advice, medicinal home remedies, or agricultural tips.

                  Source line 106

                      Part 6: Discussion and Conclusion

    I initially predicted that culinary terms like "sugar," "flour," and "butter" would naturally cluster into a distinct domestic economy or household baking topic, potentially revealing informal volumetric measurements like "teacup" or "pinch." However, the model revealed a completely different reality about the information architecture of 19th-century text. Rather than uncovering the microeconomic utility of these ingredients in a kitchen, the model consistently grouped them by their macroeconomic context as bulk, global commodities.

    In the initial 75-component model, the expected culinary vocabulary collided directly with maritime shipping logs and financial indexes. "Sugar" and "flour" were strongly associated with words like "marine," "port," and "cleared," reflecting their status as incoming cargo rather than baking supplies. Similarly, "butter" was heavily weighted alongside "wholesale," "prices," and "current" within the Boston Wholesale Prices index. The macroeconomic signal of global trade entirely drowned out the domestic signal.

    To force the model to ignore the commercial miscellany, I introduced targeted custom stopwords (marine, wholesale, boston, prices, bank) and expanded the model to 150 components to encourage narrower, highly specific topics. Despite these aggressive filtering techniques, the resulting topics (such as Topic 114) devolved into noisy, low-weight miscellany rather than coherent recipes.

    Ultimately, this negative result provides a clear insight into the historical corpus: 19th-century newspapers were fundamentally vehicles for merchant trade and commerce. Domestic economy columns were simply too statistically insignificant within the broader dataset to overpower the commercial data. The unsupervised LDA model correctly reflected the dominant economic focus of the era, proving that the kitchen could not be separated from the global market.

                          **Predictions**

        **Collection:** *The Weekly Union*, Massachusetts, 1879–1907.

                  Source line 378

                          **Discussion**

        **What I found:** I split 3,430 pages into 26,455 articles and trained LDA with *k*=30 on
        *{expression}*. Clear topics included local and county news, church items, politics,
        courts, health, schools, and market tables. Some topics are mostly OCR junk (broken words
        and table markup like *td* and *tr*) instead of one real subject.

        **Compared to my guesses:** Politics, churches, business news, and local place names
        matched what I expected. Fiction did not stand out as its own topic. Ads and repeated
        boilerplate showed up more strongly than I thought.

        **Most surprising:** Some topics track page layout and OCR errors about as much as they
        track ideas like “politics” or “religion.” Top words do not always name a theme you would
        recognize by reading one article.

        **How the techniques helped or hindered:** Splitting pages at `#` lines and merging short
        blocks cut down tiny fragments. LDA fit newspapers well because each article can mix
        topics. SQL made it easy to list topic words and find example articles. Trying *k*=20 and
        *k*=40 showed that small *k* lumps themes together and large *k* splits them apart. Extra
        stopwords cleaned up some topics but also removed useful words, so I could not drop too
        many.

                      ## Discussion

    I ran the model on 7, 25, and then 100 topics.
    I noticed that the fewer topics I added, the more everything got grouped into broad buckets, and the more topics I asked for, the more those buckets split into the specific historical events (some of which i predicted)

    - **The two biggest, most stable themes across all three runs were politics and agriculture.** at just 7 topics, 1 topic was  "farming" (corn, soil, crop, manure) and another was "politics" (bank, congress, state, president). those 2 didn't change as I added more topics later but they just got more detailed.

    - **My theme predictions became more visible at 100 topics.** At 7 topics, "national/international news" was one big group. At 100, it split into things I guessed: a Jackson-vs-Whig politics topic, a France/Paris/revolution topic (matches the 1848 European revolutions), a Mexico/war topic (Mexican-American War), a cholera topic, and a separate gold/California topic. It was satisfying to see some of those events I'd researched as their own  topics once the model had enough topics to work with.


    - **My interesting finding is how dominant agriculture topics/theme was in 1848–49.** At 7 topics, the farming topic went from almost nothing in 1834 (2 articles) to the single largest topic in 1848 (231 articles). i believe this was bigger than politics and geneal news.


    - **Railroad content showed up, but as a minor topic, not a major one.** Given Worcester's growing importance as a rail hub, I expected this to be more prominent. It did appear as its own small topic in the 1834 results (mentioning the road, line, and county), but searching for the word "railroad" had a weak recurrence.


    - I prefer having fewer topics because it gives me a cleaner summary of the corpus, but the downside is that it might blur together distinct historical events.

                      ## Part 2: Load the newspaper

                  Source line 133

                      ## Part 3: Predictions

    I did background research and found that my dataset came from Salem, MA during the eighteen hundreds when it was a busy maritime port and then an industrial/railroad town. So some themes I'd expect to find are shipping and trade related, such as cargo or arrival information, also politics in general since Salem was also a Republican stronghold, as well as railroad and transportation related themes.

    I also think that the types of articles may include ship arrivals and news about marine information, maybe some advertisements since it was a busy town, and also legal and probate notices.

    I would expect words such as ship, cargo, salem, boston, congress, railroad, etc, to be common.

                  Source line 147

                      ## Segmentation into articles

    Instead of using the full collection, which will take too long to run, I took a stratified sample of pages per year so every decade would be represented equally. I also did some cleaning before splitting:
    - remove HTML table markup
    - rejoin words hyphenated across line breaks (pay- ment becomes payment)

                      ## Part 5: Iterate

    - **Iterations**: models with 15 and 50 topics, and a 30-topic model with more words added to the stoplist.
    - **Topics over time**: the average topic proportion per year (weighted by article length)
    - **2D topic map**: PCA on the topic-word distributions, so that topics that use similar vocabulary appear close together.
    - **Embeddings + UMAP**: each article embedded with a sentence-transformer and projected to 2D, next to a UMAP of the LDA topic mixtures.
    - **Clustering**: K-means and HDBSCAN on the embeddings, compared with each article's top LDA topic.

                  Source line 580

                      ### Iterations: number of topics and stoplist

    The main model above uses 30 topics. Here I compare it with 15 and 50 topics, and with a 30-topic model
    where I added the OCR noise words I noticed in the first model (*ow, haydn, ar, us*) to the stoplist.
    All other settings stay the same. Each run is cached, so it only trains once.

                  Source line 623

                      **What changed between iterations**

    - **15 topics:** related subjects merge. War and foreign affairs become one topic (*army gen war french british*),
      Congress and elections merge (*house committee hon bill senate election vote*), marriages and deaths merge with lists
      of names, and poetry merges into storytelling (*little time old never see heart know love*). Probate, ship
      arrivals, and railroad timetables still get their own topics, so they are the most stable topics in the paper.
    - **50 topics:** subjects split into more specific topics. Ship arrivals split into three: local arrivals (*brig arr sch sailed*),
      foreign clearances (*barque cld sld liverpool*), and coastal schooners carrying coal (*sch philadelphia coal*).
      Marriages (*miss rev lynn danvers beverly*) separate from deaths (*aged yrs mos wife son*). New topics appear for Jefferson and the Embargo
      (*war british jefferson system france britain commerce*), the Civil War era (*south north southern union*),
      fashion (*hair lady ladies dress*), and domestic fiction (*mother heart father child*). But about five topics
      are tiny junk topics (each under 0.5% of the text), so 50 is starting to be too many.
    - **30 topics + extra stopwords (*ow, haydn, ar, us*):** those words disappear, and the poetry topic becomes clean
      (*thy love thou heart thee light sweet bright*). But other ad words move somewhere else (*gloves* now shows up in the
      land-sale topic), and the advertisement boilerplate topic (*public give continues provide*) appears in every run.
      Removing a few words helps readability, but it does not fix the OCR and ad noise.

    I kept 30 topics as the main model because it separates the main subjects (war, foreign affairs, Congress,
    elections, poetry) without producing many junk topics.

                  Source line 721

                      ### Sentence embeddings, UMAP, and clustering

    1. UMAP projection of embeddings colored by the topic's top LDA topic;
    3. K-means and HDBSCAN;

                  Source line 874

                      ## Part 6: Discussion

    **What did I find in the collection?**

    I found that the Salem Gazette was split broadly into two kinds of topics that did align with my predictions.

    The first type of topics are the ones about ship arrivals (topic 17: boston sch brig ship arr barque sailed), probate notices (15: county estate deceased court probate essex), marriages and deaths (8: miss aged rev yrs daughter), railroad timetables (11: p.m a.m boston danvers railroad trains lowell) and more.

    The other kind is more like prose with political rhetoric (13: state us states government people power), biographies and eulogies (28: life man whose character mind), and maybe even fiction or other narratives (18: little old man know never see).

    Beyond these topics, we also see subject topics such as ones for foreign affairs (29: french france british england king) and Congress (24: house committee bill senate), elections (12: election party whig votes), and war (7: army gen troops enemy regiment).

    These topics also change over time. In my top topic by decade table, we see a spike in 1800-1810s of the topic on foreign affairs (29) which makes sense given the Embargo and War of 1812 around that time. There are also more probate notices earlier in the dataset. In addition, from the 1820s to the 1860s the ship-arrival lists (17) are always near the top, and railroad timetables (11) appear in the 1840s and become the #1 topic in the 1850s. The war topic (7) became the top topic in the 1860s, during the Civil War. By the 1870s the paper looks more like a local paper, led by city news and fires (10), meetings, schools and churches (22), and marriages and deaths (8).


    **How do the findings compare with my predictions? What was most surprising?**

    I did have some predictions that were off, especially my predictions about common words, as both cargo and salem are not prevalent words. I was also surprised that topics that can be categorized as common prose (for example business and town related, biographies, narrative news) accounted for a substantial portion of the corpus, and there was even poetry included in the newspaper.


    **How did the text mining techniques help (or hinder) me in finding and understanding patterns?**

    *Helped:*

    - The PCA map of topics showed a clear divide of topics. PC1 separates the more fact-based list-like topics on the right (deaths, names, ship arrivals, probate, bank meetings, railroad timetables) from the prose topics on the left (political rhetoric, storytelling, biography, poetry). This showed me that a lot of the variation in the paper is about the *genre* of the writing more than the subject.
    - I also used embeddings for K-means and the K-means vs LDA comparison graphs show that several clusters line up almost one-to-one with a topic: railroad timetables (cluster 0), marriages and deaths (clusters 3 and 15), ship arrivals (clusters 20, 25, 28), land sales (16), probate (29), and poetry (14). The prose topics are spread across many clusters, which matches the idea that most articles are mixtures of topics.

    *Hindered:*

    - Several topics (13, 14, 16, 28) are made of general words and are hard to name as a theme.
    - The number of topics changes what the model "finds". With 15 topics, war and foreign affairs merge; with 50, ship
      arrivals split into three kinds and small junk topics appear. Probate, ship arrivals, and railroads showed up in
      every run, so I trust those most. Adding stopwords also only moved some OCR/ad noise (like *gloves*) to other topics.
    - The UMAP of sentence embeddings is mostly one large blob, with only a few groups clearly separated (railroad, probate, deaths, ships). The UMAP of the LDA topic proportions separates much more clearly.

                  Source line 915

                      ### Comparing the iterations to the main 30-topic model

    So the discussions above are from my main model with 30 topics. To see the impact of the # of topics and results, I also ran models separately with 15 topics and 50 topics.

    **15 topics**: with a fewer amount of topics I see related subjects merge and the big topics get bigger. For example, war and foreign affairs appears to become one topic (*army gen war french british*) and Congress and elections merge as well (*house committee hon bill senate election vote*)

    **50 topics:** With more topics, the main subjects split into more specific ones:
    - Ship news splits into four: local arrivals (*brig arr sch sailed*), ship stories (*ship capt board vessel crew*), foreign clearances (*barque cld sld liverpool*), and coastal coal schooners (*sch philadelphia coal*)
    - Railroads split into timetables and the Eastern Railroad and stagecoaches (*railroad road cars eastern stage*)

    However, the largest topic is now just general argument words (*without however subject part course opinion whether*, 8.1%), which I am not quite sure why.

                      Based on the location and time period of the newspaper that I have chosen (Worcester, 1886-1904), I predict that there will be themes relating to transportation (words like train, railroad, ticket, road), industrial development (words like building, development, trade, industry), educational advancement (words like college, rates, opening, school, students, children), sports (words like ball, team, game, score), business & money (words like market, tax, revenue, income) and local news (words like city, court, case, fire, town).                       ##Analysis

                  Source line 297

                      I notice initially that the first topic contains many html tags representing elements of a table. This is due to OCR identifying table elements. While I will clean this up during iteration, it was interesting to explore another element of this topic: the articles that are contained within it. The top 15 articles in this topic were almost all about sports, specifically baseball and hockey, and the remaining two were about stocks (railroad and New York). This suggests the most common article type in the corpus is one filled with data in the form of tables, and mostly about sports and finance. The second topic contained person-identifying words such as little, man, old, young, woman, girl, face. The topic from these words is unclear until looking at the article names; most of them were stories and articles from "Our Young Folks Page". This topic shows a very distinct correlation between words. The third topic was related to business and finance, containing words such as company, bank, business, worcester, stock, and insurance. Its top articles are almost all headlined "MEETINGS", regarding Blackstone National Bank, a historical bank in Boston. Finally, my assumption that education would be a prevalent topic was correct: the word "school" appears in 5 topics, two of them being #7 and #8. The 7th topic contains words such as school, high, work, class, college, university, and seems to be a strong and highly correlated topic.

                  Source line 400

                      Analysis:

                  Source line 503

                      ##Discussion

                  Source line 511

                      These iterations on model training revealed interesting properties about Latent Dirichlet Allocation and also this newspaper.

    Firstly, many of my predictions were correct. Explained in detail above in the first analysis of exploration, from my original exploration with 50 topics and a max_iter of 200 (325 total iterations), topics clearly identifying sports, finance, and education were apparent. Also, topics with words like police, court, and case appeared distincly. There were several unclear topics that appeared, such as a topic containing words I believe are related to nautical travel (wind, boat, first, air, fleet), and a topic complete with the first names of individuals. There was also a surpising topic #5, which was complete with articles surrounding religion. This contained words such as god, love, power, shall, must, and heart. Surprisingly, I incorrectly predicted that there would be popular topics surrounding industrial development. I did not come across a topic specifically pointing at this theme. Another very surprising topic was the one from the page for children, "Our Young Folks Page". I did not expect this to be such a prevalent topic at all, but it makes sense to be included in a local newspaper.

    I then compared two additional variations of LDA, each time changing one parameter from the original exploration: the first variation had 175 total iterations and 50 topics, and the second variation had 325 total iterations and 25 topics. I found both differences had little measured effect on the distinct topics identified, but the order of the topics varied greatly. Topics about city news, sports, finance, and god/sermons still appeared. However, when the order of topics was compared between the two variations by word matching, differences became apparent. The religion topic placed 29 for lda2, but placed 14 for lda3. The court/civil news topic placed 21 for lda2 but placed 0 for lda3. It is clear that the parameter tweaks make a significant impact in the ordering of correlated word topics. Lowering the topic count also provided some insight into topic merging. For example, the religion topic had top words of church, rev, and school for lda3 (25 topics) but top words of pastor, rev, and sunday for lda2. School was grouped into the religion topic when the number of predetermined topics was limited.

    In summary, this Latent Dirichlet Allocation technique allowed me to make insights into rough topic boundaries within this newspaper in a fairly seamless way, but only with manual iteration. Labeling topics is somewhat ambiguous, as you have to infer from your own understanding of word correlation as well as looking at document names contained within each topic. Because of the manual iteration boundary, it often takes trial and error and lots of comparison to fully understand what are the truthful topics that most accurately represent the makeup of the corpus. LDA is powerful in the sense that it easily determines word correlation and topic distributions across an incredibly large corpus. It allows you to examine overall topic structure, down to the word, without actually reading a single document. Its drawback is that topic determination and labeling often takes a lot of time, iteration, and human assumption.

    ### What I found and how to read it

    The six-topic fit with the English stoplist produced recognizable newspaper genres.
    The largest group (**31.8%**) centers on notices and business announcements, with words
    such as “notice,” “business,” “copartnership,” and “company.” A second large group
    (**27.4%**) is national politics and government, including “president,” “government,”
    “party,” and “Van Buren.” Smaller groups capture literary and general prose (**16.2%**),
    civic and institutional material (**9.0%**), shipping and port reports (**9.5%**), and
    market quotations (**6.2%**). Representative articles include “Port of Boston,” stock
    prices, “Prices Current,” and notices of company meetings and auctions.

    This mostly matches my predictions about commerce, shipping, politics, and local notices.
    The model made recurring newspaper forms easier to identify than religious material,
    which did not emerge as a distinct topic. Comparing with a run without the stoplist
    showed common prose words and OCR fragments occupying topic word lists; the English
    stoplist and added terms made the genre words more visible. Try changing the topic count
    or stoplist and compare both words and representative articles. LDA assigns each article
    a mixture of topics, and OCR errors, short ads, and reused page templates still hinder
    interpretation.

                        ###**Part3 My Preduction**:

    Boston Courier (sn84023100), published in Boston, Massachusetts, primarily spanning the mid-19th century (1820s–1860s). During this era, Boston was a premier Atlantic seaport, a major textile manufacturing hub, and an intellectual/political battleground leading up to and through the American Civil War.

    1. Anticipated Themes:
    Ships and Harbor Trade: News about ships arriving and leaving the port, their captains, and the goods they brought.
    Local and National Politics: Reports on city meetings, state laws, elections, and arguments about the Civil War.
    Business and Prices: Daily market prices for food and goods.
    Court and Legal Notices: Public announcements about sales, bankruptcies, and court cases.
    Advertisements: Ads for local stores, books, and common medicines (like cough syrups and tonics).

    3. Varieties of Articles:
    Short shipping lists, price tables, meeting summaries, telegraph news, and small column ads.

    3. Expected Common Words: boston, captain, schooner, cargo, wharf, dollars, cents, market, court, estate, notice, bank, state, regiment, massachusetts, auction, sale

                        ###Part4 Analyze

                        ###Part6 Discussion:

    **Actual Findings vs. Prior Predictions**
    The topic model identified many themes that matched my initial predictions :
    For "Shipping and Trade": As expected, there were clear maritime topics featuring words like ship, captain, port, cargo, arrived. Boston Harbor's commercial activity was heavily represented.
    "Politics and Governance": Topics with words like senate, committee, resolve, house, state, and "Government" captured the legislative and political debates of Massachusetts.
    "Legal and Business Notices": Auction notices (estate, sale, auction, dollars, street) formed persistent topics across the corpus.

    **Most Surprising Findings**
    The most surprising discovery was the massive volume of patent medicine and health advertisements. Words like doctor, cough, cures, pain, and medicine formed distinct, high-frequency topics. I originally thought news articles would dominate, but commercial classified ads and medicine remedies actually made up a large portion of the newspaper text.

    **How the Text Mining techniques helped or hindered**
    1. Topic modeling provided a fast unboxing view of thousands of articles without having to read each scan by myself.
    2. Combining word rankings with SQL queries on top-weighted documents allowed me to quickly verify what an abstract topic actually represented in the real text.

    **How it hindered**:
    1.OCR Errors and Formatting Noise: 19th-century scanned characters frequently resulted in misspelled tokens or fragmented abbreviations (e.g.th, st, co, mr), which clustered into noisy topics before adding them to the stoplist.

                        ## Part 1: Setup

    Name: Rachel Turney
