                    Source line 37

                        ## Part 3: Predictions

    The Salem Register covers the years 1802 to 1841. Because Salem was a wealthy port town during that time frame, I expect that the paper will read more like a bulletin for a shipping city than the typical newspaper. The biggest theme will likely be shipping and foreign trade, with cargo from many other nations. I also expect information about the trade embargo and the War of 1812, since both hit Salem's merchants hard. The paper probably leans toward one political party, so I expect partisan writing surrounding elections and Andrew Jackson's presidency.

    I think that there will be a lot of content that isn't articles, such as ship arrival and departure times, ads for imported goods, notices about runaway apprentices, auctions, poetry, and moral stories. The topic model can't tell these apart from news, so I expect some topics to be lists of prices and legal notices instead of actual themes.

    One specific event I expect to show is the 1830 murder of Captain Joseph White in Salem, a big trial with Daniel Webster as prosecutor.

    Words I expect to be common: ship, port, cargo, captain, sailed, arrived, Boston, Congress, President, war, sale, notice, church, God. Because this is old print, I also expect OCR errors, like "fhip" for "ship", since the old long s looks like an f.

                    Source line 53

                        # Newspaper topic model

    1. Choose a newspaper file, load its OCR pages and and split them into articles at lines starting with `#`.
    2. Merge segments that are too short.
    3. Edit the stoplist, pick a number of topics, and train with `pyrmallet`.
    4. Query the resulting `topics`, `word_topics`, `documents` and `doc_topics` tables with SQL.

                        ## Part 4: Analysis (30 topics, seed 123)

    **Date range.** The file is labeled 1802 to 1841, but 88% of the articles are from 1802 to 1807 and the rest are from January to June 1841. Nothing is from 1808 to 1840, so the War of 1812 is not in the data.

    **Matched my predictions**
    - Shipping showed up. Topics 3 and 27 (ship, arrived, capt, vessel) are ship arrival lists and shipping news, and topic 10 (British, France, vessels, commerce, treaty) is trade disputes. But together they are only about 12% of the corpus, so shipping was not the biggest theme like I guessed.
    - Politics showed up. Topic 13 (federal, republican, Jefferson, election) is election writing, though it is Federalists against Republicans in Jefferson's time, not Jackson. Topic 11 (house, committee, president) is Congress.
    - Non-article content showed up. Topics 4, 15 and 20 (deceased, estate; auction; reward) are legal notices, sales and reward ads for stolen goods. Topic 8 (thy, love, thee) is poetry.
    - Topic 2 has OCR errors like "fhall" from the long s.

    **Did not predict**
    - Napoleon and foreign war news. Topics 23 and 9 (French, troops, emperor) are about 13% of the corpus, more than shipping.
    - Single ads that ran many times, like charts and pilots for sale, a proposal to publish Locke, and cough lozenges (topics 5, 12 and 25). Each one became its own topic.
    - 1841 content in topics 11 and 18: Congress reports and insolvency notices.

    **Hard to interpret.** Topic 14 (may, every, great, public, time) is the biggest at about 11% of the corpus and looks like a catch-all of common words.

    **Failed predictions.** The 1830 White murder trial and Andrew Jackson's presidency don't appear because there are no articles from 1808 to 1840.

    Topic numbers above are from my first run (30 topics, default stopwords, seed 123). The tables above show the final model from Part 5.

                    Source line 473

                        ## Part 5: Iteration

    **Stopwords.** I added common words and OCR errors to the stoplist: may, th, shall, every, us, must, without, great, might, yet, ar, ult, inst, sch, fchr, ſhall, laft, ſuch. With 30 topics, the 10.6% catch-all topic (may, every, great, public, time) went away, but two other vague topics (man, time, never, good; state, new, states, country) took its place. Napoleon news split into diplomacy (treaty, peace, emperor) and military (army, fleet, general), and medicine ads and court trials each got their own topic.

    **10 topics (with the same stopwords).** Too broad. One topic (public, government, people, men, country) is 29% of the corpus, and Napoleon news and trade disputes merge into one topic at 18%. Ships and estate notices still separate cleanly.

    I kept 30 topics with the extra stopwords, since 10 was too blurry.

                    Source line 487

                        ## Part 6: Discussion

    **What I found.** The Salem Register file is mostly 1802 to 1807, plus January to June 1841. The topics show a paper made of sections: ship arrival lists, estate and legal notices, reward ads, poetry, election writing, and a lot of Napoleon war news. Many topics are single ads that ran repeatedly.

    **Compared to my guesses.** I was right that there would be ship lists, notices, auctions, poetry, partisan election writing, and OCR errors from the long s. However, I was wrong about the biggest theme. I guessed shipping, but shipping topics are only about 12% of the corpus and Napoleon and foreign news is about 13%. The War of 1812, Jackson, and the 1830 White murder trial are not in the file at all.

    **Most surprising.** The gap in dates. The file is labeled 1802 to 1841, but nothing is from 1808 to 1840, so several of my predictions were about years that aren't there. The second surprise was how much of the paper was repeated ads, as I just didn't realize how prevalent advertising was in this time period and didn't expect it to rival what we see now.

    **What helped.** The topic model gave me an overview of about 8,000 articles without reading them, and it showed the types of content in the paper. Reading the top articles for each topic and the topic by year table let me check what the topics meant and find the date gap.

    **What hindered.** Common words like "may" and "every" formed a catch-all topic covering about 11% of the corpus until I added stopwords. OCR errors formed their own topics, and repeated ads got a topic each and made the paper look more varied than it was. The results also depend on the number of topics and the seed, so I had to read the articles before trusting any topic label.

                        # Assignment 5: Topic Modeling

                        ## Part 2: Select a newspaper
    Boston Daily American Statesman: 494 pages, listed as 1825–1873.

                    Source line 44

                        ## Part 3: Make predictions

    **What themes will be present?**

    Before looking at the data, I see from index.json that the newspaper ran from 1825 to 1873. Boston was a booming city during this time, with wealthy merchant elites dominating the city's social structure. Because they had so much power, the newspaper is likely to focus on their businesses. Therefore, there are likely themes about "shipping and trade" as well as "construction and expansion" and "entertainment and theaters," all themes that relate to the activities of the merchants.

    **What are typical varieties of article?**

    Expanding on my original prediction, I would expect shipping notices, advertisements for goods and property, construction announcements, and theater listings or reviews.

    **What words might you expect to be common?**

    Words connected to those predicted themes include ship, port, cargo, merchant, trade, sale, land, building, street, theater, performance, and tickets.

                    Source line 273

                        ## Part 6: Findings

    **What did you find in the collection?**

    The collection contains shipping notices, railroad timetables, municipal business, and advertisements. For example, revised topic 12 includes vessel arrivals in article 32, and topic 3 contains railroad schedules.

    **Compare your actual findings to your guesses.**

    The findings partially matched my prediction. Shipping notices support the shipping and trade theme. Municipal notices about land and street widening partly support construction and expansion. The examples I reviewed did not establish a clear theater theme. The commercial content also does not, by itself, show that the newspaper favored wealthy merchants.

    **What was most surprising?**

    Topics sometimes reflect formatting rather than subjects. Topic 17 contains `td` and `tr` from HTML tables. Repetition also affects topics: articles 1745 and 1611 contain nearly identical medical advertisements.

    **Describe how the text mining techniques you applied helped (or hindered) you in finding and understanding patterns.**

    Topic modeling helped locate related articles quickly. Comparing 10 and 20 topics separated some broad themes, while removing common location words made room for other keywords. Reading the articles was still necessary to understand the topics. OCR errors, repeated advertisements, and merged articles made some topics misleading. The file only contains pages from 1825, 1872, and 1873, so it cannot show continuous change across the full date range.

                    Source line 159

                        Part 3

                    Source line 167

                        The newspaper collection I have selected is from the Boston American from 1878 to 1908. I predict that the contents of this newspaper would have themes related to business, technology, progress and reformation, as this time period included the Gilded Age that America went through and the rise of the Progressive Era. The newspaper would probably have content about rapid urbanization and the economic growth that the country is experiencing, along with the social inequality that was also present during this time. Boston specifically was a major immigration port and industrial center in this period, so I'd also expect coverage of immigration, labor unrest and strikes, and the growth of the city's transit and manufacturing infrastructure.

    In terms of typical varieties of article, I expect a mix of news reporting on local politics, crime, and civic affairs, business and financial notices, society/social pages, editorials and opinion pieces on reform issues, advertisements for goods and services and propaganda.

    For common words, I'd expect frequent references to specific places (Boston, Massachusetts, neighborhood names, and other major cities), institutional and political terms (senate, congress, strike, labor, reform, temperance, suffrage), business/economic vocabulary (company, trade, market, railroad, manufacture, capital), and language tied to progress (progress, growth, prosperity, modern, improvement).

                    Source line 496

                        Part 6

                    Source line 504

                        Running the topic model (30 topics, 500 Gibbs iterations, default stopwords plus the
    HTML-markup and high-frequency terms noted in Part 5) on the Boston American confirmed
    several of my Part 3 predictions.

    The business and reform vocabulary I predicted showed up. One topic
    (`company stock oil trust market bank money standard price copper`) reads like a checklist of
    Progressive-Era antitrust concerns. A closely related topic (`new york boston company city railroad
    chicago haven`) captures the railroad and interstate-business language I expected. Labor and
    reform also appeared as predicted, in a union/committee/meeting/labor topic and a separate
    party-politics topic (`party state league independence ward democratic candidate republican
    vote`).

    I expected a distinct advertising topic, but nothing in the top 30 showcased that, so they may have been
    filtered out before the topic model ever saw them. I also expected immigration to be a prominent, freestanding topic given Boston's role as an immigration port but instead it seems diffused across the crime, labor, and
    society topics rather than forming its own cluster.

    The single biggest surprise wasn't a topic at all was the corpus's date distribution.
    Despite covering "1878 to 1908," 98% of the 70,891 articles are from 1907-1908; 1878-79
    contributes only about 1,300. One real trend did
    show up as the party-politics topic grew from 1,729 to 2,440 articles between 1907 and 1908,
    consistent with 1908 being a presidential election year (Taft vs. Bryan).

    Sports coverage was a bigger presence than I predicted because a baseball/college-athletics
    topic and a racing/boxing topic are, combined, was the largest thematic block in the corpus,
    larger than politics or business. Text mining also surfaced two things that are really about the pipeline, not the newspaper. One topic (`td tr th table tbody thead tfoot br`) is pure leftover HTML markup from box
    scores and election-return tables that weren't fully stripped by OCR.

    Re-running with 100 topics and 200 iterations (instead of 30 topics and 500) showed broad themes splitting
    into specific, recognizable sub-topics with the single 6.69%-share politics topic became five: national politics
    (`president secretary washington roosevelt taft senator`), congressional (`bill house
    representative senate legislature senator`), Massachusetts state politics (`state committee
    convention governor massachusetts delegates whitney`), party organization (`party league
    independence ward candidate democratic republican`), and city politics that names an actual
    person, `city mayor board commission fitzgerald`.

    I also retrained at the original 30 topics / 500 iterations with `td tr th table tbody thead
    tfoot br to-day` added to the stopword list, to check whether that actually fixes the
    HTML-markup topic rather than just hiding it. It does because the dedicated markup topic is gone
    from the 30-topic list entirely, and tracking the same 1,526 documents that used to be
    classified into it, most now land where they actually belong — 876 in the baseball topic
    (`game team first boston league new ball chicago games york`), 247 in racing, 100 in the
    stock/business topic. Leftover box-score vocabulary (`po`, `hits`, `totals`) still shows up,
    but only as minor words ranked 18th-23rd inside the baseball topic instead of dominating a
    fake topic of its own. The average confidence in each document's dominant topic also ticked
    up slightly (41.6% to 42.7%), a small but real sign that removing the junk vocabulary let the
    model separate real topics more cleanly. Log-likelihood-per-token actually got worse,
    -8.74 to -8.96, but that's not a fair comparison as `td` alone was an easy 75%-probability word
    within its topic, and removing it and eight other highly predictable tokens mechanically
    lowers average predictability even though the model is doing a better job on real content.
    This run also used a new random seed, so some of the remaining topic differences are just
    run-to-run variance, not effects of the stopword change.

    Overall, the technique was good at recovering both the themes I predicted (reform, business,
    courts, society, family) and period-specific texture I couldn't have gotten from category
    labels alone (the Thaw trial, the Independence League, Standard-Oil-era trust language). Its
    blind spots were mostly upstream of the model — how I segmented articles, how much of one
    two-year window dominates the file, and how much I let it converge — rather than problems
    with topic modeling as a method.

                        **{expression}**: **{expression}** pages,

                    Source line 128

                        Part 3: Predictions
    Given the location and the time period I think some themes that will be present are economics, trading, ships, docks, finance, economy, politics, debate, conflict

                    Source line 330

                        Part 6: Discussion

    What I found:
    The first thing I noticed is that this collection isn't really 1825 to 1873. The pages only come from two years, 1825 and 1873, so it's more like two snapshots of the same paper about 50 years apart and not the time in between.

    A lot of the paper turned out to be ads and notices. Other topics covered book and school ads, insurance and company notices. Shipping showed up clearly too: Topic 4 is the list of ships arriving and leaving port, and Topic 5 looks like news that came in by ship. There was also a Boston city government topic words like mayor, a topic about fires and buildings in the city, a banking topic, and a topic for marriage and death notices. And train schedules

    Predictions vs. results:
    I predicted economics, trade, ships, finance, politics, debate, and conflict. Shipping, trade, and finance were all there, but trade mostly showed up as ads rather than news about the economy. Politics was mostly local city government instead of the national politics

    Most surprising:
    I was surprised that national politics wasn't a more prevalent topic because I lowkey thought there was war going on or 1820s was a controversial time in history.

    How the methods helped or hurt:
    Reading the top articles for each topic helped the most.

    The biggest problems were repeated ads, OCR junk, and general topics. Because the same ad was printed many times, it got its own topic, while news stories that only ran once got spread thin. Abbreviations and OCR errors cluttered the word lists.

                        ## Predictions

    I selected *The Springfield Union*, a newspaper from Springfield, Massachusetts, from the late nineteenth century. Before examining the newspaper text, I expect the collection to contain a mixture of local, national, and international news. Because Springfield was a growing industrial city, I expect to see articles related to local businesses, manufacturing, transportation, and economic activity. I also expect political coverage, particularly elections, government actions, and debates over major national issues.

    I expect common types of newspaper content to include local news stories, political reports, advertisements, business announcements, crime reports, obituaries, marriage notices, and reports about events in other parts of the United States. Since this is a historical newspaper, I also expect significant space to be devoted to commercial advertisements and short announcements rather than only longer news articles.

    Some words I expect to occur frequently include names of places such as "Springfield," "Massachusetts," and "Boston," as well as general newspaper terms such as "said," "people," "city," "state," "government," "business," "company," "Mr.," and "Mrs." I also expect words connected to politics, transportation, industry, and commerce to appear frequently. Because the text was produced using OCR from scanned historical newspapers, I expect to encounter misspelled or fragmented words that may make some topics more difficult to interpret.

                    Source line 474

                        ## Comparing Different Numbers of Topics

    I compared models with 10 and 20 topics while keeping the preprocessing
    and stopword settings constant.

    The 10-topic model produced several recognizable broad themes, including
    politics, railroads and business, religion, international affairs, local
    events, and baseball. However, some topics combined several different
    types of content.

    Increasing the number of topics to 20 produced more specific subdivisions
    of the corpus. For example, religious material, local community news,
    legal affairs, business and finance, and government-related reporting
    became more distinct. Some topics still remained broad or difficult to
    interpret, showing that increasing the number of topics does not
    automatically produce perfectly defined themes.

                    Source line 497

                        ## Discussion

    Overall, the topic models revealed many of the themes I predicted, including politics, business, transportation, local events, and international news. The models also identified more specific themes such as religion, baseball, legal affairs, and community reporting. Increasing the number of topics from 10 to 20 generally separated broad topics into more specific categories, although some topics still contained a mixture of unrelated material.

    My initial predictions were mostly consistent with the results. I expected substantial coverage of politics, business, transportation, and local affairs, and these appeared clearly in the topic models. I had also expected advertisements and commercial material to be prominent. One thing that surprised me was how clearly some more specific subjects, such as baseball and religion, emerged as distinct topics rather than simply being mixed into broader local-news categories.

    The text-mining methods were useful because topic modeling made it possible to find recurring themes across thousands of articles without reading the entire collection manually. Looking at both the highest-probability words and the documents with the highest weights for each topic was especially important: the word lists suggested possible interpretations, while the documents helped confirm what the topics actually represented. Adding stopwords also improved interpretability by removing frequent words and OCR artifacts that did not describe meaningful themes.

    At the same time, the analysis had limitations. OCR errors introduced meaningless or fragmented tokens, and some topics combined articles that did not seem to share a single clear theme. The choice of the number of topics also affected the interpretation: using too few topics produced broad mixtures, while increasing the number of topics produced more specific categories but did not guarantee that every topic was coherent. This showed that topic models are useful as an overview of a collection, but their topics still require interpretation using the original documents.
